Note: Read the Notes then Prologue first, on the left in the tabs. If you want any references or
detail on what things are, I have other docs, just say the word! (More notes on the ‘Notes’ tab.)
Pronunciation: Nia = Nee-uh
Whispers of the Inferno:
1: An Unwilling Soul
Andy stared into the doe’s eyes. It was not afraid or skittish, it was calm, and composed
itself. It may have hurt, but he wasn’t sure. It bounded away when he made a small motion with
his hand. From a young age he was able to talk to animals and interact with them, thus he was
able to understand their motivations and desires. Though he had known about his power since
he was very young, few knew of it since he did not like sharing it with others.
Because he was in the wilderness so much, he had built a hut so that he could stay there
and think on his own. He often added different materials from the forest to insulate it. By now
he had insulated it enough to protect from a blizzard, though the hut was back toward home
about half a mile. Between home and his current position, was about three miles. He knew the
way back to his home like the back of his hand, and even if he did lose his way, he could ask the
birds for guidance.
Although he knew he ought to be gardening, which was not as bad as his other chores,
he enjoyed the company that the fowls and beasts of nature brought. Gardening was caring for
things that grow. He loved the autumn leaves that crunched beneath his feet and the cool breeze
ruffling his hair. Autumn was his favorite season because of the beautiful colors, and he was
glad that winter came late in their little valley.
He did not enjoy his chores greatly, but they did give him something to distract himself
from his troubles. He wanted so badly to see the world and abide by his own rules, however, and
his father had been in anguish ever since the death of his mother.
No one knew where he was; he liked it that way. He wanted to do as he pleased with the
animals and interact with them, otherwise to listen to the sound of silence, with only the soft
flickers of movement and sound interrupting his thought, or inspiring it. When he was outside,
surrounded by only nature he felt as free as the animals he understood so well.
He spotted a sparrow flitting sporadically in the sky and reached out to it with his mind.
He greeted it with a small invitation to join him. The invitation was polite and kind. The bird
glided down to him and landed on his finger as he was extending it. The joyful, flitting, little
bird brought a smile to his face with its quiet piercing voice. Although the bird could not
communicate with words like he could, it could still understand his feelings, and he, it’s feelings.
He heard the bird's thoughts, which would have been confusing to any other. Its feelings
communicated, What do you want?
I only want your wonderful company. You bring me joy.
The bird did not answer, but chirped loudly in response.
He set the bird down, but remained connected with it. He sighed and thought in the
presence of the sparrow’s mind. Fast fluttering thoughts passed through Andy’s mind, just like
the movement of the bird, and they shared a moment together.
He was sixteen years upon eighteenth, and eighteen was the traditional age that you left
the home and went to discover the new things that you may. You were considered strong enough
to go off on your own at that age. Andy was always very cautious with how he interacted with
people because he did not want them to think that there was something off about him; his
power had always been in his life and it always left a doubt of the people around him when he
used the power. He did not want their judgement. The only person he trusted with his power
was his sister, and though she did not know about it yet, Andy planned to tell her soon.
He knew though, that his father would want him home.
They lived in a valley tucked away to the southwest of the kingdom of Anira and held not
much sway in the dealings of the world. Their home was three miles away from the rest of their
humble village, and they had a small abode upon a large tree in a perfect clearing. The tree had
stood for at least three hundred and fifty years, a dynamic symbol of strength and integrity.
Their parents had built their house themselves fourteen years ago when they arrived at the
village. No one would think to cut the tree down over its long life because they admired it as a
sign of strength and endurance. Like my mother. He thought.
His mother had died twelve years ago in the damp spring months. No one anticipated
the sudden death and it was tragic for many who had known and interacted with her. He
remembered her as a person who would snatch the slightest chance to help or serve someone
even if it was a minor thing; she considered service as a privilege. What sort of privilege was that?
Andy was always eager to learn how to view the world from her eyes.
As he wandered back to his home, through the green of his backyard, the passing of his
thoughts occupied his mind.
Upon arriving home, he did not go to tell his father, but instead, to the garden. There
must be a few late growing snacks he could pick. Maybe the beans, unlikely for the carrots, as
the foliage was still short for most of them.
The ground stayed moist because of the humid air and morning mist that drifted in the
air. Andy enjoyed the thin hours of morning crisp, because the sound of the waking world
pervaded the air. The hours of morning were still young, and the sun had just peeked over the
mountains to the east. The golden light spilled over the mountains with blinding brilliance, a
stark contrast to the slow, dead hours of night, and the joyous chirp of a whippoorwill welcomed
it, bringing a small smile to Andy’s countenance.
In the garden, the green beans were ready, and it was probably time to harvest the
potatoes. Potatoes were Andy’s favorite and very filling. He would often take them along into the
wilderness and bake them out there in the forest.
Sometimes, he would eat a small bird, though he made sure to give them a courtesy of a
quick death, as was his custom for any animal, if he chose to eat meat. He knew the benefits,
and he knew the necessity.
He had a harder time eating animals because he could understand their minds. Talking
with the animals got him in touch with them through a connection of the mind, which is why he
could understand their feelings. They knew that their own sacrifice was necessary for the
survival of him and the villagers, some were willing while other species were not. He always
remembered to thank them, when possible, for their sacrifice.
He looked around the property to find his father, and to see what was needed. To
understand what significant item needed to be completed, he knew his father would contain the
answer. His father never seemed to consider the inconvenience it caused Andy and tear him
away from his animal friends. He would much rather spend time with them than his family.
Andy had a sister and a brother. He felt a certain protectiveness over his sister because
she was young, and in his eyes beautiful; like a budding flower stretching eagerly to find the sun.
He knew his older brother felt the same way even though he and Andy never ceased to argue —
so it seemed. He trusted his older brother in an emergency, but with the less important matters,
he did not want his input. His sister, Nia, was fifteen years old, while his brother, Lairn, was five
years older than he was. It was a strange gap, but never answered.
“Son?” The voice came from the wood shed. His father must have heard Andy’s
movements, and approaching footsteps.
Stepping into the shed and greeting his father, Trevar, Andy said, “Yes, father?”
“I need you and your brother to harvest the potatoes,” his answer was blunt.
“Yes. Where is Nia?”
“She is down in the village with Liam.”
“Ah.” was all Andy replied with. Andy didn’t doubt Nia was also doing other things, but
Liam was a trustworthy, upstanding person. Liam liked Andy’s little sister and never treated her
rudely or insulted her. Liam, in Andy’s opinion, was a true friend; a friend who wanted to help
everyone, as well as being humble, patient, diligent, and most importantly, a man with strong,
steady morals.
Andy headed into the house to find his brother and retrieve the tools for harvest. He
climbed up the stairs to the house and found the tools first. The treehouse had five stories; one
was for each of their rooms and the other was for the dining room and a stairway to the root
cellar. Lairn’s room was the second to the top of the tree. His was on the third floor of the
treehouse, while Nia’s was on the top and Trevar’s was on the bottom. Trevar thought the
organization was the best to keep everyone safe. If intruders got past Trevar, they would have to
fight the brothers. The brothers were to protect young Nia in any situation.
Though intruders were not expected or common in the small town, there were still
known to be thieves among the growing village. Sometimes outsiders would attempt to steal and
make off with their stolen items, and violence had been used on several occasions. It was a good
set up in Andy’s opinion, and he was a light sleeper, so he would hear any creaking in the old
wood.
When he found Lairn, they went out to the garden without question to harvest the
potatoes and other vegetables that might have grown late and were now ready. They did not
communicate much except for the simplest words. While they were digging and gathering, they
did not find many other different crops that had grown, except a small bunch of carrots with
dead leaves and some beans. None of the potatoes were rotten which was a positive
improvement to their condition. Last year they had rotted because they had been given too
much water. Some of the other crops diminished in their size but they were still edible. Even if
the potatoes were the only thing that were edible, that would be totally fine with Andy; no other
vegetable tasted better. They also had two horses and a goat; the horses were used to bring
materials to the town in order to buy or to sell extra items.
When they were finished with the work, the results from the crop took two trips from
each of them to transport everything. They stored the food in the root cellar below the tree and
Lairn returned to his room where he had been asleep. He did have a good reason for his
weariness though, he had helped a blacksmith in the village transport tools to Alabak, a town
across the mountains to the east. He had never ridden a horse as long as he had that week, and
riding was their method of transportation. The saddle turned out to leave him more sore than he
originally thought before he accepted the task and a strange sickness had consumed his waking
hours.
Andy had heard sneaky creaks in the night, for Lairn was sleeping even through the day,
and woke up at night.
Andy was eager for his bed too and night was not far off. Night meant food, and they had
their supper late in the night every day. He had not had lunch with his family so he was eager to
see what was to be made. They each made a meal in turn. Trevar made three meals a week, Lairn
made two, and Andy and Nia made one. Nia was going to start making more meals at her age,
and she enjoyed the culinary arts, so it was no hard thing for her. She never complained about
making meals or going into town, because that meant she could see Liam.
***
When the night approached and Nia had returned from town to prepare supper, as it was
her turn to do so, Andy offered to help her, mostly because he wanted to talk to her about Liam.
Her hair was a copper color and her face was slim but always bore a smile. Her eyes were brown
and she stood five feet off the ground, almost a foot shorter than he was.
Their small stove sat by the door to the tree house and was divided from the wood with
many layers of clay to hinder any damage to the tree. There was a counter in the opposite corner
of the room which was fifteen feet long and half that wide made of solid stone. The dining table
was on the opposite side of the room that the root cellar was, close to the door. The chairs
around the table were made by hand and did not look as stable as they were.
Nia had already prepared most of the meal that consisted of chicken and a side of the few
beans and carrots that Lairn and Andy had collected earlier in the day.
When he offered to carry the vegetables to the table she answered, “No, I will, you
remove the chicken from the oven.”
She often posed her questions in the form of a demand, not really meaning to, but only
expecting you to answer, as proper courtesy required. She took the vegetables to the dining table
and Andy did as she had asked.
The chicken was ready after a few minutes and Andy removed it from the oven while Nia
prepared the table and ran to fetch Lairn and Trevar for the meal.
When they had all gathered to the table and dished up their various portions, Trevar
asked Nia what happened in town.
“I bought the chicken for a good price of four silver coins and then I sold the excess
produce we harvested last week in September.” Nia relayed happily.
Trevar replied, “You visited Liam,” It wasn’t a question.
She only smiled, and Trevar did not push the matter further. They ate the rest of the meal
in silence and washed up for bed after everyone was content. The silence hung without so much
as a breath to be heard by the others who sat around the table.
Nia thanked him for his little help and made sure to give him a hug as she always did.
They all went to their beds, eager for the rest that awaited them. They slept peacefully that
night; Andy was asleep as soon as he settled into his bed and drifted to sleep absent of dreams.
When Andy woke, he was eager to return to the woods. He invited Nia to join him as he
often did, but she politely declined the offer and told him she needed to attend to a few things
and run a few errands. She promised to spend time with him in the evening.
Even though she had refused his invitation, he was still bubbly to be in the midst of the
tall pines and maple trees. There were many other trees aside from the two, but pine and maple
were the most common in the valley. Despite the meager variety of plant species, the animals
were abundant and varied. There were deer, rabbits, many birds, foxes, squirrels, the big cats,
and many others besides.
In the transformed forest of red and orange, the animals seemed to have a decreased
attention span and did not spend much time in his mind. Usually the animals were eager to
share their mixed thoughts, feelings, and memories. Confused, Andy wandered in the forest for
multiple hours, wondering what had caused the animals’ strange dilemma. What could it be?
He asked a fox bounding through the forest for an answer to all of the commotion in the
other animals' minds. All it told him was that something was coming, and even it seemed
hurried. What could this thing have been? It could be a big storm, but winter storms did not
come for another eight weeks. It was possible it was a rain storm, but rain was not common here
in the Autumn months. Andy thought as he wandered. He dismissed the chaos of the creatures'
minds as a coming rain storm, though it continued to bother him throughout the day.
Later, Andy walked to their humble village of Glendon to search for Nia who he knew
would be about the houses. Glendon was named after the first man to set eyes upon the valley
and to found a village in their humble little vale.
He wandered through the houses of all sizes and looked for any useful items. He
probably wouldn’t buy much, if any, because his family was poor and his father may be upset
that he wasted money. He may not think it was a waste, but if they did not absolutely need it, his
father would give him a scolding.
He found Nia in a small shop that sold dairy products as well as products from other
animals, such as eggs, pork, and beef. She had just paid for some food and turned to the door to
leave; she usually did the shopping.
When she saw him, she smiled and approached him. She held a basket with a few items
in it. She usually wore a pack, but in this case there were some fragile items. The eggs were not
prone to cracking in a basket nearly as much as they were in a pack.
“I bought some ingredients for you and your cooking tonight, do you want my help? I
had an idea in mind.” she said, white teeth flashing as she spoke.
“What did you get, and what was your idea?”
“I got you some eggs, fresh milk, and then some beef for tomorrow,” she looked up at
Andy, beaming.
“Thank you,” he returned the expression.
They returned to the tree, stored the ingredients for later, and went into the forest. This
time Nia was willing to come with him and Andy wanted to tell her of his power. He trusted her
enough to expel the secret and spread it to her. She was true and would not give away his
personal information unless she had his permission, maybe not even then.
As they walked through the pines and maples, they talked about many things, from
memories years ago, to the week before, or even what they wanted as they grew closer to being
an adult. Nia told him of a time a squirrel climbed into her room from the window, and he told
her of some big potatoes they had harvested that were as big as his head. Overall, they were very
open with each other, sharing what they would during the evening.
“Liam told me he had to leave the village to go to Alabak. He hasn’t told me what for yet,
but I think he will,” Nia stated suddenly, then continued with a low smile, “He’s honest, he will
probably tell me. You should hunt with him, he knows a good deal about the plants and
animals.”
“Maybe we can when you go into the village tomorrow. I will come with you, and maybe
I’ll ask him.” Andy replied.
“I could… Of course you can!” she threw her arms around him. Though it didn’t surprise
him with her cheery nature, he was still surprised by the suddenness of her actions.
He chuckled, “I love you too; never could ask for a better sister.”
She didn’t reply, but he felt her hug him tighter. They held for a time and then released
and sat in the middle of the clearing they had stopped in.
“I like to sit here and listen to the animals, hear the birds, the river…” Andy trailed off to
give effect to his words.
“They’re beautiful aren’t they, the leaves, the sounds?”
Andy sighed, “Yes, they are.”
“What do you think we will be like in the next few years?” Nia asked, a sincere and deep
question; the future was like looking into a rippling pond. You could always see something you
might do, but that is when uncertainty descends upon you, that certainty fading as a stone is
dropped in.
They both stared into the sky and contemplated the question. Who could predict the
future? Andy thought as he sat, deeply, calmly, and swiftly. He was reminded of what the fox had
told him not too long ago in the day. The fox said, ‘Something is coming.’ How could it know what
was in the future? He concluded to ask the fox when he saw it again. He could know which fox or
other animal he had spoken to by what their mind felt like, he could also ask other animals, the
birds perhaps. If the birds did not know what was going to take place, who would? The birds
saw everything below their wings.
2: A Willing Soul
After a few hours, Nia stood up and asked him what she was brought out in the forest
for. Andy led her to the clearing he had been in the day before, which was not too far and asked
her to close her eyes and stay quiet. When she did as he asked, he gave another command.
“Alright,” he said softly, “Listen.”
As she nodded, Andy reached out his hand and touched her head. She laughed a little,
probably thinking it was strange, though she kept her eyes closed. As Andy opened the door to
all the animals' minds and allowed their thoughts to flow to Nia. When he was fully focused, he
heard a gasp escape from her lips.
Smiling, he asked, “What do you think?”
She was breathless for a few seconds, but caught her breath to say, “Well… It… How? It's
awesome! How?”
Removing his hand from her forehead, Andy replied, “Ever since I was small, I had it.
The power to talk to animals, that is why I am always in the forest. You learn so much from the
minds of animals and their thoughts. They are so lively and… joyful,” Andy could think of no
better description.
She kept her eyes closed for a time and when she opened them, a high smile appeared on
her face filled with joy. He was glad he had a sister, and it made it better when she was always
cheery and loving. “You remind me of an animal's thoughts, lively and joyful.”
Her already bright face glowed even more. She started asking him questions about some
of the animals' thoughts, like ‘what did the rabbit mean by, “share the tree”?’ or ‘What does
‘river flow’ mean.” He took the time to explain all the details to her because he knew that she did
not understand the animals thoughts as well as he did. Their conversation continued and they
talked for multiple hours; she never seemed to run out of questions, though he enjoyed
answering them anyway. When they were finished in the clearing, a cold breeze blew through
the trees in a psithurism. The sun was setting, they had forgotten, the days were getting shorter
and darkness swiftly approached.
“We need to go.” Nia said.
“I didn’t realize it was so late, I am sorry.”
“Nothing to forgive.” she said with her little smile on her face.
They both knew what the other was thinking; what will father think? They hurried back
the way they had come. They were three miles away from home and they did not think they
would make it before the sun set. Andy knew the sun was descending over the mountain even as
he ran with Nia. When the sun’s face extinguished, a cold sting touched Andy’s cheeks. A
snowflake? Andy thought. Nia must have thought the same thing, for she risked her footing to
glance at him in a questioning look.
Andy thought more and more as the snowflakes frequency multiplied and the wind speed
accelerated. He knew they would have to find a place to hunker down until the storm was over.
Andy recalled his hut he had built over the years. He thought it was the most viable option. Why
had the snow come so soon? What did the fox mean? Why does nothing make sense right now? Too
many questions buzzing around in Andy’s mind and the darkening sky made it hard for him to
see and focus. He couldn’t remember where the hut was. Was it to the left or right? He couldn't
remember.
The sky was dark above. Through the trees. And the snowflakes grew larger. More
abundant. Hard for them to see. They stumbled through the dark. The cold jabbed at their
vulnerable arms and cheeks.
A voice came into Andy’s head, Forward and left.
Nia seemed to hear it too, and they paused in their movement. When they confirmed that
they had both heard it, they did as the mysterious voice commanded, even though they did not
know what it was or where it came from. It was their only choice. They had to run for another
ten minutes in the blizzard before they reached the hut, but it was there.
In the hut it was still cold, but even as the lay shivering there, the small abode rose in
temperature. Andy felt terrible that Nia had to endure this with him, but he knew she would
dismiss it as something of no consequence.
Outside, the snow was already piling up against the hut. What way to stay… The animals!
Andy thought. They could always keep each other warm and they would be far more
comfortable. Immediately he reached with his mind to the animals, big and small, hostile or not,
he wanted to try all that he could. He was not about to make a folly mistake, especially when he
had the potential to stop it. He felt the foxes, rabbits, birds, and even large cats. Some of the
animals' minds he refrained from touching because they were confused and possibly irrational.
When his call was complete, animals poured into the hut, even a cougar strode in.
Nia backed away against the wall of the hut as the animals entered but Andy calmed her
with the soft touch of his mind. She recoiled at his touch, and even he was surprised, he had
only reached for her mind out of habit and instinct. He turned to the animals and asked them to
surround himself and Nia also requested that some of the smaller animals lay on them. The
animals’ lively spirit was full of energy and warmth, and they also adapted to the climate to stay
warm. The hot breath of the animals, and their body heat as well, warmed the hut very quickly
and the air filled with the smell of the animals’ previous meal.
Nia was still nervous but she held her ground; she would be brave when she grew into an
adult. She would do what is necessary wherever she was. Andy reached out and touched her
shoulder in order to calm her further. She smiled at him and then put her arm around him so
they could warm each other. Andy liked that about her: she always looked to unite with someone
else so they could help one another, and she always had a smile drawn across her lips.
As they sat with the animals and warmed the other, the animals started to purr or snore
in their varied ways, and the consistent sound and rhythm calmed them. The voice entered their
minds again…
When soft and small become so great.
When the night descends a dramatic rate.
When you slow and forget the uprising date,
You sink, you sink into your fate.
***
When Andy opened his eyes, only a fox remained laying contentedly on his lap. All the
other animals had left the hut, and Nia was still sound asleep on the floor. Outside there was
about a foot of snow on the ground that looked so cold from inside the warmth of his shelter.
The blizzard had ceased and no clouds were seen through the entrance of the hut. Nia would not
want to travel in the cold snow, she had never liked the cold and he knew she would be
reluctant.
It was then that Andy remembered the horses. Though he did not know the direction to
guide his thoughts, he knew that a bird could fly over the trees and easily transmit the
information as to the whereabouts of his home. Without waking Nia, he gently picked up the fox
and set it next to her, telling it to keep her warm as best it could. When he looked out the
threshold of the hut, there was a wide snow arch on the ground in front of the hut because the
wind hurled the snow from behind the shelter that blocked the flurries from reaching the inside.
Andy looked to the sky but did not see a bird, nor anything moving, not a sound nor a
stir through the trees, only silence. The silence seemed so heavy, like the snow that ceased to fall
was pressing down upon him. He reached with his mind to the sky and outward. It took longer
than he expected; it might have been because he was still dreary. He searched farther but not too
far before he sensed the mind of a bird soaring over the trees. He would have thought there
would be none about, but this one seemed curious as to what the snow was at this uncommon
time.
Friend, will you do me a favor?
What? The bird's voice-thoughts were clear.
Do you know the location of the home I live in?
I know the land.
Which direction is it?
North.
Thank you, my friend.
The exchange was short, but Andy got what he needed, and from its thoughts, the bird
seemed eager to get somewhere.
When he left the bird's mind, he cast his thoughts north for a distance, and focused on
the horses. He wandered for a time before he reached the horses. Over so far a distance, his
mind was not strong enough to contact both of them, so he only contacted one, and even then,
his voice was faint in the creature's head.
As he withdrew from the horse's mind and returned to his own, he heard Nia groan and
sit up. He returned to her side and sat there, by her. He did not want to tell her until she asked,
or the horse came. She leaned against his shoulder and let out a short sigh.
“What would I do without you, Andy?” Nia questioned, her eyes focused on the snow
outside.
“I value your love, trust, and company.”
She chuckled and said, “I trust, love you, and value your company too.”
“That's why I care about you.”
“What about Lairn, you don’t trust him, but I know you care for him. You don’t like to
admit you are wrong or admit when someone figures things out about you.” she said as she
pulled away from his shoulder and looked at him expectantly.
“I trust him when it comes to an emergency. He and I never got along…” He paused and
looked expressionless at Nia, her sudden comments left Andy hastily fumbling for proper words,
“I’ll work on it.”
“Good, we all need to learn.”
She replaced her head upon his shoulder and he leaned on her head, both staring blankly
out of the comfortable hut. Everything had happened so quickly as to be unmanageable, and the
exhausted time had passed so fast. Vague, anxious, questions from the night before bounced into
Andy’s disoriented mind as well as the beautiful piece of smooth poetry recited by the
mysterious voice they had heard twice, returned. What did the voice mean? No matter how much
he thought about it, he could not divine its meaning.
While they waited, they continued to talk about dim aspects they could improve on, and
advising each other how they could become their greatest in this life. To Andy, Nia gave sound
advice that seemed to lightly contrast his, but they seemed to balance each other out. Nia also
continued asking questions about how he communicated with the animals and what some of
them meant. Andy had her make a short promise not to tell anyone, so it would be their secret,
not to be spread rampant.
When the horses finally arrived, Andy briefly told her what he had done while she was
sleeping and how he had called the horses. She was still greatly fascinated by how he could talk
to the animals through feelings and emotions. While they unpacked blankets from the
saddlebags, a thought occurred to Andy then. If she could talk to animals like I do… His thought
trailed off shortly and a smile touched his cracking lips at the idea of the possibility.
They mounted the calm horse, Nia on the front, because she was shorter, and Andy in
the back, because he could see over her to steer at the same time. Andy was the one that often
did the driving of the horses because he loved to be near animals the most out of the rest of his
family.
He asked Nia to try to reach with her mind to the obedient horse but she was unable to
contact the animal, even though she tried desperately several times. She seemed somewhat
disappointed and Nia wanted him to help her. He told her to focus her thoughts into a string,
one emotion at a time and then let the contact flow and then think in words what you wanted
them to understand. When she tried the next time, she succeeded and almost shouted when she
felt the horse’s mind. Her reaction startled him, but he congratulated her and continued o her to
experiment with the other animals of the woods and she had a great deal of success but was
frustrated when an animal did not want to talk or they were too far away for her to communicate
with.
They continued talking about the things they had discussed the previous day — what the
future would be like for them. Even though Andy could answer all Nia’s questions and give a few
simple responses, the same haunting, though not unsettling, words from the poem echoed
through his mind. When he pushed the thought away, he found himself more calm and able to
focus on a single subject.
Upon arriving at the house Lairn was looking out his window and when he spotted them;
he ran down to greet them. They unmounted and tied the horses to a tree for the time being
while waiting for Trevar and Lairn to flood them with questions and demands.
“What happened to you, were you caught in the blizzard?” Trevar asked them with a
worried look.
Many of the questions regarded how they got the horses to find them and they found
themselves evading the true answer as it would reveal Andy’s ability as well as Nia’s. They did
not want to reveal it because they were afraid that they would think that they had hid the ability
from their trusted family. Hiding those details was no easy task, to lie and especially to family,
even if it was Lairn.
Trevar and Lairn’s questions continued throughout the day as they went about their daily
routine and chores. Nia was not going into town but would tomorrow as she had told him Liam
was to leave the morning after next. Andy reached to Nia's mind and they communicated during
their chores but the feeling of her mind calmed him, and they conversed about what the poem
and mysterious voice might mean.
Time passed, and the day bound up, tying the day into a bramble rather than a neat knot.
All the while Andy and Nia talked. They wondered together, and shared memories of both
amusing and confusing times, then Andy re-iterated what the voice had said and it gave both of
them pause.
Even though Andy received a restful sleep, his dreams were filled with fierce wolves and
dark forests that bowed in on him. They seemed so real to him, but he never could wake up, only
let them scrape by. They seemed to tear viciously at his mind, his own thoughts and memories
turning against him. Desperate as he was, he only escaped the torment when Nia shook him and
invited him to go to the village with her to meet Liam.
The morning had come soon. Though Andy welcomed the reprieve, the time also seemed
to brush so smoothly by. Like a cold breeze, leaving a harsh sting on cheeks.
Andy accepted and they set off for the village in order to find Liam. They talked a great
deal more, and like the day before, they talked about the future, the blizzard and the animals.
When they arrived at Liam's home, Andy discovered that, as soon as he stepped through the
door, he had no parents. His home was built of logs and sat upon the ground, unlike their tall
tree they lived in. Liam answered the door and invited them in, smiling at both of them, then he
sat them down to learn about Andy, and Andy wanted to learn more about him.
“I’ve heard much about you,” Andy said bluntly but not unkindly, “You, though, also, may
know something of me though I don’t know how much.”
“Yes, I know, I know what you wonder, the first thing anyone wonders when they enter.
‘Where are your parents?’ they ask… They are gone, maybe dead, but I can’t be sure,” a spark of
sadness sprang into his eyes, but he took a deep breath and said, “I have done my best, and the
others would not abandon me, they have supported me as best any of them could, made my
world lit and bright.”
“I am sorry,” Andy paused, “If there is anything I can do, let me know.” Andy chose to
offer his help, not because he really wanted to, but rather, because Nia was there and she would
appreciate the offer.
“Thank you, but I will probably be leaving soon,” He looked at Nia, “Even I do not know
why I am leaving, but I am needed in Alabak, for work or other reasons.”
“Well, that is strange, but there is probably a good reason. When you return, I should
think you two will be close friends.” Nia said.
A small smile spread across Liam's face and he returned his gaze to Andy saying, “From
what I have heard of you, you deeply enjoy nature, I love it too. I have had to hunt for myself
many times, and I have become accustomed to the greenery, as well as the vibrant oranges of the
Autumn season.”
Andy glanced at Nia, recognizing the color of her eyes, and they seemed to glow, as
Andy’s aura had when he used his ability. She wanted to share with him her new ability. Then a
thought entered Andy’s mind. Was she able to do this before?
He touched her mind with his and asked her, Will he believe us? I trust him if you trust him.
He can be trusted, but I wonder what he will think.
Even though their decision was a fast one and somewhat whimsical, they had decided to
share with Liam what they willed about their corresponding abilities, though they did not tell
him when Nia had discovered hers.
When they finished explaining, Liam asked, “ Could you touch my mind, like you can
touch each other's minds, or…” he trailed off strangely.
Andy simply answered, “Yes.”
“How far can you contact someone?”
“I can contact someone about five miles away, but I have to know where to direct my
thoughts.” Andy answered, only disclosing the information because he trusted Nia’s judgement
of Liam.
Andy thought Liam was trustworthy, and knew how to work because Andy could see his
muscular arms. He had a cheery mood about him and he did not seem to complain about
anything. He was tall with dark brown hair, his face was broad and he seemed to think things
through. Andy discovered that Liam had a good memory and was able to identify almost every
animal in the woods, as well as every plant.
The day passed swiftly, and soon, they had to return to the Treehouse to eat and clean up
for sleep. They were not only in the village that day, but also in the forestry that was behind
Liam’s house. He told them the names of all the plants as well as all the trees, more various than
behind Andy and Nia’s home. When he could not identify a plant, he asked Andy or Nia to ask
an animal. There were not many animals about, because of the blizzard’s sudden appearance,
and the blanket of snow that remained on the ground.
That night the snow hit them again, but this time, three feet fell. They woke the next day
to the crippling sight, denying them comfortable passage. When they looked to the sky to see
what clouds had thrown the snow, once again no clouds could be seen. Trevar suspected
something was going to happen but he never suspected more snow. If anything, it should have
been rain that fell. It was not so cold yet. They were able to stoke a bright fire that warmed the
house very quickly. They had a warning, the cold breeze. Whatever the weather could have been
changing to, the first few feet were not a pleasant sign, but surely an effective one.
Something was at work.
Nothing was amiss in the temperature of the morning, but Andy had never experienced
anything like the storm. Even in the winter they had never had such heavy snowfall. The biggest
snowstorm he had seen was seven years ago upon the day of his birth. They had gotten a little
over two feet that day.
Liam was not able to depart for Alabak that morning so he aspired to gather extra
materials trying to make the trip in the thick snow. Nia was helping him collect the items, and
he was left to himself to do what he willed. Their routine for cooking and many other things
around the house were sabotaged by the snow and they were busy taking care of the animals
that may have been injured or perhaps be sick.
Andy had nothing to do, and the forest clearing would be hard to get to, even if the
animals were present. He decided to reach with his mind for the fox he had communicated with
a few days ago. He wanted to ask about the storm, and how things were faring in the woods.
Since he had no better idea of how to spend the day, he reached toward the forest. He wandered
in the forest searching for the fox. He knew that it could give him an answer, because it was
probably huddled in a den at the moment.
When he finally felt the fox’s mind he asked his question, though it took him hours to
find.
I sense more, some… thing in the wind, the fox told him.
Andy thanked him for the information and then sat in thought of what he had heard.
What else, a worse storm? Or was it something different. What was he doing sitting idly here, he
should be preparing to face what this coming foe was, in nature or human form it may be.
3: Fire Scorch, Ice Sting
Awakening from his sleep, a week later, from a dream that he had, no other storm had
struck them. The dream was not frightening, but it had a feeling surrounding it that was
unusual. Precipitation had fallen for the past three days straight but not as snow; heavy, dark
rain clouds spirting with lightning moved slothfully over the sky.
He climbed out of his bed and looked out the small window that opened to the right of
his bed. The rain hadn’t ceased and showed no sign of doing so, the clouds in fact seemed to
darken every passing day. What is this unnatural downpour? Andy thought as he gazed out into the
torrent of drenching rain. Descending to the kitchen, he found Lairn and Nia, along with Trevar
who was preparing breakfast. He was cooking something warm, and Andy always liked when his
father cooked, it was always good. He liked the other meals too, but he was always surprised
with his fathers culinary skills. He could always turn a typical meal into a work of art, they
needed that on these dreary, and quite literally, blue days.
When they had finished eating and everybody got their fill they knew they would have to
go chop down another tree for firewood. But in the rain, the log would be wet and not take
easily to a fire, but only smolder. If they get the wood cut while they still had dry wood, then
they could warm the wood that remained wet so the water on it could evaporate.
Wet wood smelled good. Even though rain could be dark and dampen the mood, it could
also bring new unearthly green and more color into the world. Rain seemed to bring out the
flavor in the world. Andy imagined what a meadow would look like after this storm, where the
grass would have plenty of water.
When Lairn, Travar, and himself went out into the forest to select a tree, a dry, dead,
standing one, they found several that were perfect for the size of the small wood stove in their
home. The whole process, finding multiple trees, chopping down, and sawing every part to the
perfect size took about five hours. Their wood shed was now very full with three small trees
chopped into logs. They even had a small stack in front of their stove for the water inside to
evaporate out.
It was about two o'clock after they had completed their task so they were able to come
into the home and smell something that Nia had prepared for them while they were at work.
There was not much talk, while they ate and nobody attempted to strike up a conversion either.
Andy was not as disturbed as he was the past few days and everything seemed mostly back to
normal, except for the torrential downpour that had been going on. They could hear the rain
pounding upon the roof and it was constant and unforgiving. Nia had to travel into the village to
buy supplies and left the men in the house.
All the while they were in their home listening to the thunder above them combined
with the roar of the rain. Then the rain ceased.
Crack! Rumble.
They stopped talking as they were, eyes wide and glanced around each other. What was
that? It was the lightning! They all seemed to think the same thing at once because they all got up
to look out the front window. As they looked, the trees were on fire, hot, scorching fire that they
could feel even where they were inside, it should have been too wet. It was no longer safe. They
had to evacuate. Even so, the fire spread, hot enough to catch the drenched trees ablaze.
They had to escape the valley and they had to do it quickly. The fire was spreading and it
was even downing trees faster and destroying the forest. Andy longed to help the animals in the
forest, but they probably smelled it. They were on the move. His family was not in a good
position to get out of the valley and the forest in front of them, toward the town and fire also
spread to the forest behind them.
All the memories would be destroyed. He with Nia, the hut, everything. His animal
friends, the trees, the beauty of the best. The autumn orange may be covered, but not without
consequence. Around them already trees had fallen. The trees seemed to sag, to droop with
sadness, like they had feelings. They knew their life would not be the same after this event. They
had to abandon their village, their home… Nia! She was still in the village!
Andy reached with his mind through the blazing wood. He touched another familiar
mind, he received a small bit of information from him, I started this… Nia was trying to help a
child and that was when she sensed his presence. She connected with him and communicated,
Your safe! I saw the lightning. You need to get out of there now!
We are, but we have to climb out of the trees first.
No, stay by the river, I’ll find you, call the animals.
He was going to reply, but she cut him off by withdrawing from his mind. He then
realized what the cost of reaching so far meant he had to leave his own mind. He was standing
in a clearing where he usually met the animals and interacted with them. How was he separated
from his family? Where were they? He had to find them. As he thought about where to start, he
could not begin to imagine what they might have done, everything was jumbled in his mind. He
did not even know what the name of the next day was. Then something familiar happened, a
breeze.
That was not a good sign, the snow, the disaster, and he didn’t remember where he was.
The voice returned inside his head, and he suddenly felt very tired.
Winter water grows too cold.
Aging in the forest old.
Dare to take the challenge bold
Before your freezing heart is sold.
Be strong tonight, you must fulfill your fate.
He slept.
He dreamt of many things that he could not make sense of. He did not even remember
the pain of tiring thoughts he had been experiencing while he was conscious. Sleep helped, it
eased his mind and cleared his worry of pain he felt for a while, though sleep could not fully
suppress it. With conscious thought inside his dream, he thought something encouraging. When
we give our all, we grow so tall, but when we fall, we at least must try to crawl. Move, for what? Move for
others.
After the thought was complete, nothing remained, not even a scrap of thought
connected to a dream.
When Andy again gained consciousness, he sat up. He was in the same place, and the
sun was out. The snow was still on the ground but he was on the side of a clearing under a tree.
The snow was not so thick and nothing seemed to be amiss. When he looked above though,
there was a slightly disturbing sight sitting there. A falcon was sitting perched on a branch
above him in a tall pine tree. Something seemed strange about the bird, it was watching him too.
Even after the bird flew away he still felt watched.
He decided to see what was left of the village that surely would have been caught by fire.
Even though he knew that the town was most likely disintegrated, he still wanted to see it.
There is where he would decide what to do next. What had become of those who were traveling in
the snow? Were they okay? What about Nia? Was she and Lairn and his father safe? He silently begged
to the snow that they would be alright.
When he arrived in the village, it was as suspected, black, charred, nothing. Andy turned
his eyes away for he could not bear the sight of his village on the ground, flat. The trees were but
shadows of their beauty, and looked more like ghostly spires. The trees and houses were not the
only thing, a few villagers lay with one another. Some were dead because a beam had fallen on
them or some were killed by the great heat of the fire. What was to be done?
Seeing the faces of his dead friends, pained him, friends. He needed a friend. Then he
remembered, “...I should think you will be close friends.”
The memory reminded him of Liam's stutter. He had trailed off when he was explaining
the reason, the ‘I started this…’ too was Liam. He is responsible for this! Argh!
“You!” he yelled into the sky, “You betrayer! You are responsible for all these innocent
peoples’ lives!”
If anyone was around they would have heard it from a mile around, the mountains would
provide an echo through the valley. He was furious, he wanted revenge. He needed to find that
treacherous murderer, with the blood of the innocent on his hands. He would start in Alabak, if
he wasn’t there, he would go to the forest, before the real winter cold hit. First he had to gather
provisions to set out on this quest.
He first went to his home to see if the horses had survived, though he knew that they
both could be dead, especially if they were in the barn. When he arrived he was grateful to see
one had broken out of the coral and was grazing in a small patch of grass about three hundred
yards away from their home. The horse was healthy and appeared not to have injuries but he
could tell it sensed his presence and was skittish. He reached to it to reassure it and to help it
understand the circumstances he was in.
When he had fully prepared the horse with only supplies he could find lying about his
house he set off immediately to avenge all those who had been harmed, and Nia. He did it for
them, and Liam would pay…
He had only taken with him a few small things such as sleeping materials and food he
had found still intact down in their root cellar, potatoes, and more potatoes. All he needed
besides that were a few seasonings that were stored in shelves of the cellar, which he snagged
for use. He did not take much more except an extra change of clothes that were in the
saddlebags from a previous venture.
His horse trudged through the snow that was still two feet deep in multiple areas. The
snow proved to be a bigger obstacle than was anticipated, but they fared well as they traveled
along the river. The river followed the forest up until the river hid itself in the mountains. He
could see the mountains clearly when he approached them, and he did, he was very close to
entering the mountains. He would have kept going, but the darkness made it difficult to lead the
horse in the right direction.
When he reached the mountains the next day, he thought he heard something in the
smaller trees of the mountains, though he could not be sure. He continued to think about the
tragic experience and his loss of a good friend. His family was always on his mind, and he could
never stop thinking about them and what would become of them. When he was not able to
dispel the thoughts by the end of the day, he had to let the thoughts that troubled him go, he
knew it was no good to dwell on his inconveniences.
As he prepared for sleeping, and as he set up his small camp, he allowed himself to be
grateful. Grateful for his ability to talk with the animals and the experiences that were
consequences of the desire. He knew that without his ability he would be very helpless in the
forest, and if he were not raised away from other people, though he saw them, that he was
accustomed to being alone.
Well that is an improvement. It was the voice Andy had heard from many days ago. He
knew it now and always thought about its advice. He was surprised to be hearing it now, even
though he wanted to hear the voice again. The voice felt so wise and was never saying
something unless it was necessary.
He looked around to see where the voice was coming from but he could not spot the
source of the voice. After a few moments of confusion and thought, the voice spoke again.
Above.
As Andy looked up, there on the tree were two orange dots, reflecting in the eyes of the
creature looming in the tree. There was a flapping sound, and the falcon from the day before
descended into the firelight of the small campfire Andy had built.
When Andy could see the falcon clearly, he asked with voice and mind, “Why have you
been watching me, and what do those words of yours mean?”
I am sent to look after you by another’s accord whom I cannot utter the name yet. You are the
one.
“Why are you a bird?” Andy said, not at all what the bird expected
All I am allowed to disclose is that I was once human; I once walked upon two legs as you do.
“What then, do all of your words you shared to me mean? Why was it at that time that
you did?”
You need my view, you should never be discouraged, you can do more than you see inside
yourself. You do not understand the length of your abilities yet. The stretch into other, more dangerous
paths, you must be cautious.
Transitioning from talking out loud Andy asked, Do you have a name?
Of course, my name is Eradris, it was translated into fire speech, but in your tongue, it is only
Eradris.
How old are you? I am curious about what you are doing here.
Questions, well… I am old enough. One more question, then you must sleep. You are the one who
needs the rest.
Alright, what is the commotion coming from, what and why is it happening, it's unnatural
especially in this valley. Winter only begins in late November.
I was young and human still, I did not understand at the time, but I have had much the loved and
hated thing to reflect on past scraps of memory. When evil entered this land, it was still young and the
ground tilled only once. It took hold, but nobody recognized it. This land was once filled with a great lake
covering most of the land. It was fresh and clean water, filled with much life and spirit. The evil being
swept away the lake and dug out a drain with his mighty power, in case the water ever returned. The
bottom of the lake became inhabited and uncovered new landscape. Forests began to grow, towns built
and people spread throughout the land. Not only they, but the evil servants took a foothold in the land.
They stayed secret at first, but soon emerged from hiding. Soon after the far northeast towns and cities
were taken over by the might of the army evil had created. And so the land has remained for an untold
amount of years, thriving but falling with the whims of the world. When I could see what was happening,
or anyone, time was short if not spent. I had no way to even stop the evils’ spread. And so its hold is now
very strong over the oblivious land, very few remember, and those who do struggle with the forces of the
established empire. Some say the evil takes the shape of a shadow, and some say fire, though I do not
know which.
Ah. Was all Andy could think to think.
Sleep.
Andy did what the wise falcon asked, and was soon consumed in a deep, restful sleep. He
had no dreams and he knew he was safe.
On the next morning, Eradris followed Andy through the day and they made it far. They
covered more distance and reached the town by nightfall though a feeling shimmered over them
as they traveled. Andy knew it was not Eradris, nor was it the loss, but something else was
watching their steps.
Though they reached the town, they knew they could not stay for long, or the town
would discover him. He wanted to stay, and perhaps he might for a short while to see if he could
find his family. If he did find them, he had to make a choice, would he pursue his quest for
revenge or stay comfortably with his family? He wanted to see Nia, his father, and he even
wanted to see Lairn. When he thought about it, he got a headache and his gut churned with the
decision. What would Nia think? What would father think? If they knew he was mad enough to
go on a vengeance quest on his own, they would turn it down.
You must not trouble yourself with this. If they come, you will then have a decision before you,
now the future is what lies ahead, but it is not here.
It took some will, but Andy was able to force the thoughts out of his mind. It proved to
be harder than it seemed. Nothing seemed easy at the time, but one thing was clear, tomorrow
was still waiting. Andy wanted to gather supplies if he was going to leave but he did not have
much money. He only had enough on him to pay for an inn and stable. The Stables were well
built and the inn was warm. His quarters for the day, or week, were small but suffice especially
when only one used it.
Many of those who stayed at the inn were drunken with some drink and bore strange
smells about them, their breath filled the air, putrid and rotten. His father had taught him never
to widdle his wits to where he couldn’t use them, to always think about the consequences of his
actions before he took them. He was glad then for his family, and only then realized how much
they taught him.
When he went to bed that night, he thought of his family. He was only going to stay for
three more days before he left. If his family had not come with the other villagers by that time,
like he knew they would if possible, then he would depart. A plain lay between him and his goal,
the forests of Rafaril. Rafaril was the most mysterious place he knew of, he may find answers to
his questions inside. There were tales about the forest, tales of a cave that let a person know
whatever he or she wanted. It was a rather whimsical idea and irrational decision.
Eradris had stayed outside the town and did not want to be seen, as the people would
wonder what a falcon was doing above the town. He also did not want to be near the clamor of
the people. The town was busy and a constant flow of people moved from place to place. Andy
was only focused on sleep, however, and his eyes were shutting on their own.
No dreams assailed him that night, but at the end of his rest as he ascended out of his
rest. The thought was reassuring. It was only meant for giving him hope. It calmed his weary
mind and lifted his heart; never had he thought as he did at this time. Hoping for things that
cannot be done, we push on, and then we will fall, everybody.
In the morning, Andy wanted to gather the needed materials for travel, he needed to get
more food, extra blankets, and other materials for weather that would possibly hit him. He did
not know if the blizzard would return to the land either, or if the valley was all that was to be
caught in its chilling air. Even though Andy was hopeful, the thought of the return of the snow
disheartened him. He did not mind the cold, but it would slow his travel, were it to return. He
also needed to find a weapon smith, though he did not know how to use one, never had there
been a need to.
Weapons could cost money and that was the thing he did not have enough of. His horse’s
saddle bags may contain more, but hardly would it be enough. Weapons were very costly,
because the metal to make them was scarce in the western regions. No one could get enough
metal to forge with unless they bought it. Smiths had to be wise with their money or they would
lose it. Without a weapon, he was bound to be seriously injured on the trail, if not die in his
quest. He could also visit someone who could teach him the basics of a sword, which he wanted.
Training would also cost him though. He had no way of getting money, then he had an idea,
though it might be the only route.
He made his way to the stable to see if anyone was there, tending to the few horses that
were there. There was someone. He had a long beard and slim face that matched his body and
his scrawny arms. The man looked up and saw him, “Are you looking for something?” the man
said as he stroked a horse’s neck.
“What is your price for one of these beautiful things?” Andy asked.
“Which one?”
Andy looked over all the horses in the stable, spotted his, and asked the man about it.
“Well, that fine horse would be about sixty gold.”
“Well, can I sell him to you?”
“Hmm, you are clever,” the man paused, “yes you can, though I do not like to be
outwitted.”
Andy nodded to the man and thanked him. The horse was something he had ridden
many times, and he had interacted with it many times before, so he wanted the horse to be safe.
“I will drop the price by five gold if you will see to it that he is well taken care of.” Andy
told the man.
“Oh? What would you do that for?”
“Well, even if you tend to all the horses, I want to thank you if you do, and persuade you
if you don’t.”
“You have my word.”
They quickly made the exchange, and the money was in Andy’s hand. He would have
enough at least to buy the weapon, but he did not know how much he would have left for the
fund of training. He would also have to find someone who trained, or at least knew how to fight
with his chosen weapon. He was thankful the man did not ask why he had chosen to sell the
horse, and he could now start searching for a weapon smith.
He searched through the day and looked throughout the medium sized town. He did not,
however, find an actual smith. He had found someone though. When he found it, he was eager to
ask if he could get him a weapon. He knocked on the home, and he could smell a fire and hear
the sound of the hammer. He knocked again, and this time the hammer stopped. There was a
thumping up what sounded like hollow wood stairs, then the door opened.
Standing here was a normal man with common height and bulking arms, from pounding
metal and working a bellows for years of his life. The man looked him over, raised an eyebrow,
then grunted and motioned for him to follow. Andy followed eagerly and made sure to close the
door behind him. Off to the right, wooden stairs descended into a room with a stone floor and
many tools lying about. There were also a few forges that sat around the room along with
different sizes of anvils, from eight inches, to three feet. Andy had never seen something that
large made of solid metal. He looked at the walls and was surprised to see many swords and even
an axe or spear. Andy would have thought the smith would need to sell the tools to be able to
purchase more money, unless he was wealthy enough to buy what he needed, whenever he
needed.
When Andy’s attention returned to the smith, the smith was leaning against his anvil,
looking at Andy, most likely expecting him to ask for a price or a different question.
Andy then asked, “What is your price on these fine weapons? I know that metal is
scarce.”
“You would be lookin’ at a price of about twenty five gold, you be right in sayin’
materials are scarce. Hardly the finest weapons, but surely sturdy,” the man gestured to a sword
on the wall, “Have you ever used a blade.”
“No, I have not even touched one.”
“Hmm, let's find you a sword, eh?”
Not knowing what else to say, Andy followed the man to the sword he had gestured to.
The sword was very rough, but the edge was sharp. The man removed the sword from the rack
on the wall, and handed it to Andy, instructing him on how to hold it properly. The sword was
one handed and easy to wield. Andy thought the sword felt nice in his hand.
When the smith asked him how it felt though, he scoffed and beckoned for the sword.
Andy, trusting the man’s experience, handed the sword back to the smith to return to the rack.
They continued testing the swords until they found the proper one. When they found the right
one, it felt comfortable. The sword was not the most balanced, but the hilt would not slip out of
his hand, and the smith also approved of it. When the smith said, the sword was worth less,
Andy’s mood jumped high, he needed to save the money.
The price was twenty one gold coins, and they settled on the price because Andy did not
know how to be polite or tactful when trying to negotiate a lower price, he was not good with
any words. Andy was not one to slack though, he was off to find someone who could train him,
so he asked the smith where he could find someone. The smith told him to search on the other
side of the town, telling him that there were a few soldiers on that side of the town that might be
willing to teach him how to use a blade.
He thanked the smith and set off to do some training, if he could find someone of course.
He had no idea what being taught with a blade was going to be like because he had never had
schooling, except for that which his parents had given him. He knew that he would not enjoy
taking orders or obeying someone else, he never had. He was one who was shy of those he had
not grown up with, so he may be distracted by those factors too. He knew not what the teacher
would be like, which is why feared.
Once he was out the door, though he knew he should not procrastinate, for he felt
rushed, even though he knew he could have a week to complete all that he needed. The
uncertainty was the problem, he did not know if he had a week. He needed the extra training as
well, as much as possible. He did not want to be caught without any at all, or else he was sure to
be hurt on his journey. Fighting with a sword was a useful skill as well, so he was eager to learn.
He walked through the street for a while, trying to get to the north edge of the village,
where the smith said he was likely to find someone willing to teach him. The constant flow of
people was overwhelming, and Andy had to backtrack several times to find a street that wasn’t
filled with people. The commotion and noise was one of the things that he hated. He was not
much of an extrovert, and the noise gave him a buzzing headache.
His head buzzing with the commotion, he emerged into an area where only a few people
were moving in. He looked past the buildings and saw grass, so he headed in that direction, to
those houses. He knew that people would be less likely to move near the soldiers quarters. The
soldiers were used to giving orders. He did not know for sure if soldiers were there, but he
assumed they would be leery around them.
He breathed in deep and let out a sigh before walking up to a door and pounding his fist
against the door. He heard footsteps immediately after his knock had succeeded. They were
small feet, and he also heard a voice behind the door. What was this house, a family? He
expected a swordsman to be secluded from a family. He did not want to disturb the family, but
he also knew it would be impolite to run off without them seeing anyone at the door. He also
knew that his very presence may be annoying to whoever was behind the door.
The door opened to reveal an exhausted female face, a mother of multiple children, Andy
guessed.
“What do you need here?” She asked him bluntly, but not impolitely.
“I was told people on this edge of town know how to wield a sword. Does anyone here
know?”
“My husband does, why?”
“I need to learn the art, only the basics though.”
“I’ll go talk to him, wait here.” As her sentence stopped, she closed the door.
He waited for a few minutes before he heard more noise behind the door. A man opened
the door wide and peered at Andy. He didn’t know if he should speak first. The man examined
him, then appeared as if he were thinking to himself for a few seconds. He too closed the door to
a crack and then said something before his face appeared in the door again. This time though,
he came out of the house and motioned for him to follow.
“What brings you here? Why do you seek the blade?” the man said as they walked behind
the house and into a small shed-like building. Before Andy could answer, the man continued,
“You’ve come to the right place, you know. I am the only one with a specific place for sparring.”
Andy did not answer, for his question had been answered and he was not going to make
a poor decision to ask an unneeded question. He also wondered why he hadn’t seen any soldiers.
He had expected to see a few, but he was not expecting none at all. There were many soldiers in
the land, they had even come to their village before. When the soldiers did come though, they
usually were not there for long. The soldiers at his home were not as rude as those he had heard
from travelers in the land that had actually seen the land. He knew soldiers had been at Alabak
in the past but now where were they?
As they continued their walk to the shed, which was farther away than it looked, Andy
thought all of this.
When they were there, the shed looked as if it had been worn and without paint, though
Andy could tell it was only fading. The paint was not the only thing that was faded, the name
was too, so he could not make out what was on the top of the shed.
When they entered the shed, the inside was much bigger than it looked from the outside.
The shed was not as dingy as the outside and had a few weapons on the wall, but the weapons
were not as masterfully done as the ones in the smith’s home. The walls were polished wood and
beams lay across the top.
“Have you a weapon?” the man asked.
“Yes, here.”
“Good, we had better not stall. What is your name?”
“I am Andy.”
“Let’s begin.”
After that, the man started teaching him about different fighting styles and had him
work through a system or series of swings with the sword to see his fighting proficiency. His
style of fighting was best as slashing and stabbing, so he chose to teach him in the category from
then on. They met blades only once, and even then only for ten seconds. The seconds seemed to
slip by like water and it seemed as if Andy could not retain any information in the seemingly
short time. They taught and learned, and the day was almost near three o’clock when they were
finished. The man had asked Andy how long he wanted to be there, and he only told him two
days longer.
The man was to plan accordingly to Andy’s plan and then Andy would be off in the
morning of the second day. Andy needed his revenge, and he was not fond on waiting for the
time to come.
Next, he wanted to explore more of the city, though he was afraid to because of the mobs
of people. If he had gotten there at night, and the city was already bustling with people, he did
not want to think about how many would be wandering around now. He was not about to wait
any longer, especially with the time it took him to find the man who taught him. No, if he was
going to accomplish his goal, he was going to have to leave earlier than he wanted.
When he immersed himself in the crowd, they automatically started pushing and pulling
him, left and right. He needed to flow with the crowd, or else he would not get anywhere. He
was going to be pushed wherever the people decided to go. When he finally gained some
leverage, he wanted to visit a specific shop he had seen. The shop had caught his eye, and he was
curious. His head hurt more than the first time, and he wanted a rest from the constant pull and
push.
Andy managed to get to the shop, and get inside before the people pulled him away
again. He saw only a desk. The walls were bare except for one picture, a picture of something
green boiling in a pot. He did not like the look, but his curiosity was still high, he also wanted to
see what people were outside the streets.
As he examined the room behind the desk, he heard a deep noise, coming from a shelf
behind and above the desk. The sound was like a soft purr, but not quite the same. He reached
out toward the shelves and explored them for any sign of life. He found it on the right of the
desk. It was about the size of a cat but it was glowing more than any regular cat would, with
more life and rigor to its spirit. The animal was aware of his presence, but was waiting to see
what Andy would do.
Andy decided to withdraw his mind and try to talk to the animal. He could still hear the
clamor of noise behind him in the street.
“What is that purr, you there on the shelf? Are you able to speak aloud?” Andy asked,
though not fully expecting an answer.
“I can speak, but how did you find my presence?” The voice was calm and not raspy, but
sort of squeaky. His words seemed to linger in Andy’s mind, and it unsettled him, but he stood
his ground.
“I can sense you, and hear your calming purr.”
“What then, do you want with me?”
“I was only entering to know what normal things are like, outside the raucous out there.”
Andy motioned to the door.
“Ah, perhaps you could help me.”
“With what do you need help with?”
“I need the fire of Avlaan. It was stolen from me. You must return it to me.” The cat’s
voice rose and fell with his emotion of words. He wanted a flame of Avlaan. What even was that?
“I do not know what that is, please enlighten me. I am most curious.”
Emerging from his hiding place, the cat walked on the divider between the shelf and
casually made his way into a back room while walking on the ceiling. Then Andy followed,
wondering how the cat accomplished its strange feat. He wanted to be able to do that, but he
only had the power to talk with animals. Andy was still dumbfounded as he entered the room
and saw the cat sitting like a king on a beautiful crimson chair with golden tacks pinned into it.
“Well,” the cat began, “What is this knowledge you lack?”
“What the fire of Avlaan is.”
“You are strange, everybody knows. Very well, I shall fill you in. Nine hundred years ago,
a fire entered this world with one purpose, to destroy the world through pain and torment in the
worst ways possible. Though he could not do this, his power did have a limit. They spread
different fires throughout the land so they could search for power. These fires burned, as you
sensed my mind, also do they burn. They live. Whoever wields the power wields the will to
control the fires region. Lucky are we, not to have a fire governing us. I need this fire, it wields
great power, power enough to strike at the evil. We want our land back, and it wants our land.
We must fight like this, it is the only power great enough to break the original fire. Collect these
fires, and bring them to me, find me in the land and we could destroy the evil forever.”
“Where are these fires?”
“In the four regions, not so different now, they used to be great regions of splendid color,
and our time grows short. I fear the evil is very close to finding the power it needs. Nine
hundred years is a long time. But not if you have to search the nine regions of Alabak. The Ice
Region is the largest and most significant. The closest to achieving its goal though is the forest,
also very big. Nine regions and nine lands. They are breaking apart, and that is his goal. You
must find these fires. Bring them back to me.”
“Well, I know you see better than I do about these matters, but if I find them, I will do as
you say.” Andy said, not knowing if he should deny the cat.
“Fair all the well, well, never tell an ill one that they are well, they will not take kindly to
that remark.”
Andy nodded and left the back room without looking back, nor did he want to. The
conversation was strange and the cat was strange, never had he seen a verbal speaking cat. He
had only talked to one through his mind, and even then, their minds were twisted and different.
Seeing one that knew so much though, was even worse.
What was this flame? What were its other flames? The nine regions? The Ice Region? He
had no idea. He could not make sense of something nonsensical. When so many parts and
motivations were behind it too, worse. Everything just had to crash down at this time. If he was
going to keep his word, then he could not run about without sufficient understanding.
Knowledge was power, and power is not always used for the most wise things.
As the next few days flew by there was no sign of a wandering village or something
strange, he listened to the people, but nothing came about his family. He continued sparring and
training with the man, and got better every day though he knew he had not even scratched the
surface. He bought food on the second day after sparring, though he knew he was not going to
stay much longer. He was going to need food for the journey into the wilder lands, especially
without a horse. He needed a pack also to carry his provisions and keep his possessions
organized.
He watched the horizon, and at the end of the fourth day he saw people. The people were
hard to see from a distance, but he did not doubt they were the villagers. Andy wanted to greet
them, but he had to clarify that they were those of his village. When they were closer, a half an
hour later, Andy recognized many of them. Sixty arrived, though others may have died in the
fires and decreased the number. The amount unsettled him, for he knew surely that more than
sixty escaped the fire. What could have decreased the number, perhaps the cold?
No, they had more endurance than to die there in the cold. It had taken the villagers a
week to reach the town though, while it took Andy only three days. He could be mistaken, but
he believed he wasn’t.
As the villagers came into the town, he wanted to see his sister, brother, and his father,
his hopes were high too. They came in and he did not see his family in the first twenty, nor the
second. As the third set of twenty came through the gate, he saw his sister, Nia. She was looking
better than she did in the cold, and Andy wondered where the snow had ceased. Andy was
suddenly hit with the decision again, would he reveal himself to his family or leave, knowing
they were safe. Or were they? An icy, stinging thought entered Andy’s mind, would he risk their
safety or did he dare bare them on his shoulders as an extra weight? Then a burning thought
scorched his heart and he suddenly wondered where Lairn was, and Trevar. What had become of
them? Nia was alone when she touched his mind in all the commotion.
Andy found himself closing his eyes, both sides, leave or stay, fought with each other,
along with the scorch and sting throwing details into the fray. He struggled with his thoughts
and then suddenly opened his eyes, decision made. It was not either of the sides, but somewhere
in between. He would not leave Nia but he would be departing; soon. He wanted to ask if she
would go with him and they could bear the weight together.
He, having made his decision, had another question poked at him as he made his way
through the crowd toward Nia. Where was the traitor, the murderer, that Liam? All this pressing
on him, he met Nia and she had a smile on her face as she always did. She brightened even more
as she saw him. They greeted each other and walked with the crowd, even though they would get
pulled apart for seconds at a time. Andy led her to the inn, and though she had never been in an
inn before, she knew what it was like.
They entered the inn, and made their way up to the room Andy had rented. When the
door was closed and they were in private, Nia hugged him and said, “I am so glad you're alive! I
just hope Lairn and father are all right. I reached your mind and I felt it, but it was slow and
confusing at the time, I could not enter it to speak with you, and you seemed to take no heed of
my presence.”
“Yes, I have something to ask of you though it may be forward.”
4: State of Conscience
“I need to go to the forest, we will be safer there, though I do not have a horse for us to
ride on now, I had to sell it, though I did not want to do so, will you come with me?
She took a deep breath, “Yes, I trust you, but what is there? Is there a city or town there
to stay in?”
“There is a town, but there is also danger on the way, we must not be separated, for it
could be fatal. There is danger we have not faced before, like in stories.”
“We can learn, though I will admit I'm afraid.” She said releasing him.
A scrap of poetry formed in Andy’s mind about Nia, it matched her personality and her
traits, she was constantly doing it in the hard circumstances.
Let your light shine
And give it as a sign
For the wind is strong
And the storm is long
He just then realized how much he actually cared for her, he did not pay attention to
detail well. The night approached swiftly with the shrinking days and the dark grew
dramatically. Andy did not want to be caught the next day without food, but he also knew that
he didn’t want to get caught in the night crowd either. The night crowd would push him to
where he wasn’t able to see, which could be dangerous.
Eradris had not spoken to him through the past days, but now, Andy wanted to ask him
something about the cat and also about the nine regions it had spoken of. He thought the bird
would know more if he told him about the cat and what he had learned. Eradris was wise and he
knew much, so he probably knew about the matters throughout the land, and he was a bird too.
He resolved to ask him when he knew exactly where he was so he could touch his mind.
They slept in the same room that night and Andy let Nia have the bed and he slept on
the floor. They were not uncomfortable, but they were disturbed by the fact that they were going
to leave everything they knew behind, for a quest of answers. Everyone wanted answers, but not
everyone had the strength to pursue them. Knowledge came with strife, power came with
