the top. The interior of the shack was not as he expected. He thought it would be some sort of
tool shed, but the walls were mounted not with racks, but with cubbies. Feathers littered the
ground and the soft gurgle and coo of pigeons greeted them, and a puff of feathers flitted down
from a box on the left.
At first glance, there appeared to be about ten boxes, but there might’ve been more. Two
skylights made out of glass filtered in light through the top of the roof and feeders on the
ground where some pigeons stopped their feeding to glance up at the two men entering. Their
small black and orange eyes considered them; some waddled away, but most resumed eating.
The man stepped close to each of the boxes and examined them to check for something
he knew not. He wondered why he had them; he had never used something like this back home,
so he wondered what it was for. He wanted to ask, but his question was soon answered by a
small smile, as well as a nod from the man. He pulled a small paper out of the seventh box he
stopped by, and unraveled it. Liam could not see the contents of the small note, but assumed it
was a message of some kind. So he does have interaction, Liam thought.
The man quickly checked the remaining five boxes, but he did not remove anything else.
He had somewhat of a displeased expression crawling across his face. It was not prominent, but
he did not try to hide it, he seemed troubled, and hurried.
Narrowly pushing past Liam, the man exited the small coop and left the door open for
Liam. Curious, Liam followed the man to see where he would lead him. When Liam was sure of
his destination, The Hill, he thought about returning to sit by the fire, but wondered what the
man wanted. If he had anything planned for the rest of the day, Liam did not know it. When
Liam started to veer toward the fire, the man made no objection or action to call out, so Liam
assumed he was content with the decision.
As the man went into the hill, and Liam to the fire, Liam wondered what was depicted or
written on the paper to trouble him as it did. He had interaction, but with who? Liam settled
comfortably in a wooden chair that squeeked as he let his weight down.
Bringing his left leg up to rest on his knee, Liam looked up at the sun, which was high in
the sky, past noon he suspected. He realized just how much time they had taken in the morning
to fish.
As time passed, Liam showed no sign of hunger, but stared up at the sky’s shifting colors,
from cloud to fluffy cloud. He examined the landscape on the mountains that he was surrounded
in, and it reminded him of another such mountain ring he had been in. Liam suddenly wondered
where he was, what mountain range, or even how he got there. A small wave of homesickness
washed over him as the mountains also briefly reminded him of his home, the valley he was
from.
27: A Woeful Waring
The day was starting to get old, and Liam was tired of sitting in the same place, so he
decided to look in the surrounding forest. It would not be too hard to get lost, he thought, in the
enclosed mountain bowl. He went to the edge of the trees, listening for any sounds of animals or
other signs of life. As he strained his ears, he heard the withering of stone to water, a waterfall’s
relentless pounding on the rocks.
Liam followed the sound, listening to the crackle of dry long-fallen leaves beneath his
feet. Occasionally, a stick was broken underfoot, and Liam expected something to come out of
the forest after him, but it was silent all around. The forest itself seemed hushed; the very sound
of the waterfall seemed muted. Though it got louder as he approached it, it was like the clack of
rocks shifting under water.
Finally, past a short turn, there was a small clearing around a small pond and cascading
waterfall. The water divided in the middle because of a pointed rock protruding from the small
cliff. Bushes obscured the view of where the water flowed to get to the point of the fall, but Liam
suspected a spring, or maybe the fall was only a little run off.
He stared at the mist that was created by the waterfall, and so short an impression came
in his mind that he almost missed it. The urge to see if anything was behind the waterfall. The
quick possibility somehow sent shivers down Liam’s back, and he wondered if it was a good
idea. What else do I have to do, I can get a little wet. He walked to the pond where the ripples sent
by the falling water created a broken reflection of him in the water. There were stones in the
pond that Liam could use to get to the fall, but he again looked at the water, and again, it gave
him pause.
He plunged on, ignoring the small and fearful voice in his head. There was no immediate
danger, but the curiosity to know what mystery was behind drove him. When he stood at the
brink of the cascade, he wondered if this was somehow a dream. What if he was not here at all,
but still in the forest where he had fainted. It was not a comforting prospect, but still, he ducked
his head and plunged it into the falling, and soon he realized very cold water. Would a dream feel
this way?
Part of him wanted it to be a dream, and some of him wanted it to be real, but his
thoughts seemed hushed, like the silence of the forest. Through the waterfall, was a tall but
narrow cave, a passageway with a little light at the end. It led steadily upward, and the stone
looked cold, but the hush deepened, and he could not even hear the water behind him anymore.
Cautious, but eager he started the ascent to the light.
What he would find at the top of the tunnel seemed like a boulder boding to crush him.
As he got higher, the tunnel seemed longer, but the desire, stronger to reach the end. Step by
weary step, he strained to reach the top, but it only got farther away.
His mind was filled with confusing rushing thoughts, all the more frantic as he kept
going. At one point, Liam looked back on the path, but it seemed endless too. He continued
forward, toward the light, in which he stumbled and fell. Fear filled him and he suddenly found
himself curled in a ball to protect from unwanted pests. Thoughts of terror, of his past, of Andy,
of Nia, of home besieged his mind.
It was like a thousand battering fists pounding at his inner self, threatening to flatten
him if he let them in. Without fully realizing it, Liam’s mind shattered, and his thoughts were
like a broken window, strewn to the past, to inside, outside, then, of the future. A peace settled
over him as an involuntary thought rushed through his mind, like an autumn breeze stirring the
leaves. His mind was slowly reconstructed, as it seemed, and Liam was finally able to stand
again.
He looked at the light, squinted, then, when his thoughts had fixed themselves in place,
he broke out in a run. He felt that the farther he ran, the harder it became. Like he was being
pulled back into a slingshot only to fly farther backward than he had run forward.
He stumbled again, but this time, when the fear clutched him, he turned it inside out and
used it as fuel as if something was chasing him from behind. Running faster than before, though
his legs shot fire with every step, he let out a yell. It was getting closer, it was getting brighter, it
was getting clearer! Faster he ran, and faster still, cramps sent pain through his body, and a low
lying throb ran deep in his head. His vision started to cloud, and then all the feeling in the world
ceased, and he suddenly felt like he was floating, though he could see his feet secure on the
ground.
Before him lay the land, stretching far, stretching wide, but it was not his land. It was
dead, blackened, charred and consumed by some menacing, dark power. He was distraught
though as he picked out the detail of mountains, mountains he recognized. He himself stood
atop the peak of a taller mountain. The land was his land, but corrupted. What power could bear
to destroy something so beautiful.
He looked all around, but the only green was in the bowl. From his point there, he could
see the drifting smoke rising from the fire. The pond down below had an unearthly shimmer,
and Liam realized the time. The sun was behind the mountain for the bottom of the valley, save
for the V’s that the mountains created. The sun had acquired a fiery luster spreading a rusty
smear across the sky, like the embers of a dying fire.
Weary, drained, empty, Liam looked back down the tunnel, but now, more humble than
before; he could hear the echo of the water reverberating through it. Head hung low, he silently,
dejected and downcast, trudged down the tunnel, slow, but moving.
Before he took his first step, a similar sound to the waterfall met his ears, but it was
more subtle and consistent. He looked back at the land, and although it took him a while, he
spotted the source, it almost blended with the setting pale-blue sky. At first, to Liam, it looked
like a wave, but it did not move. It was like looking through a giant clear glass barrier filled with
water.
Liam wondered at it for a short moment, then ducked his head into the tunnel and
proceeded down the path.
It did not take him as long to get out, not nearly, but it still felt that way. He heard the
waterfall, felt the mist, and knew, wherever he was, no hope remained.
What could have happened? What could have made that happen?
The water drenched him to the bone as he stepped out of the fall. The sun was setting
and a small tail of smoke was rising from the smoldering bed of coals that was their fire. Stars
had already pierced the blackish-blue sky, and he wondered if the man was still awake and
aware.
Through the forest he walked, and even the trees and their leaves seemed to be hiding
from the dark of the night. The trail seemed so bright however, and the moon had started rising,
casting a pale light among the stubs of grass and short bushes. Though it was ghostly and a little
grim, the light gave a certain contradiction to the rest of the night.
Liam considered kicking a small rock, but it seemed pointless. Even all anger that might
be within him was absent. It also seemed meaningless to be angry; what could it accomplish?
Either way, if he was to keep to his own standards, it was pointless to despair.
It is easy to come into pain, but when pain becomes my focus, pain becomes my life. So, what is
my life? Liam thought, a sudden wave of revelation sweeping the driftwood away brought his
weary and wandering thoughts with it as it made its retreat.
Quietly by the fire, the man sat as Liam approached it. There was a small log burning,
one that the man must have put in recently because surely there would only be the bare coals.
“I know what you saw, news has also reached me.” the man said simply.
28: Pattering of Fate
“How could it be,” then quieter, to himself Liam whispered, “how long have I slept?”
“You slept?”
“I fainted, it was uncontrolled and I… I didn’t know why.”
“Ah, hmm,” the man looked down for a brief moment, seeming to recall something.
“Where?”
“The forest, it must have been… I don’t know the name. I’ve never been told its name.”
The man’s eyes went wide, but this time, the expression lingered. He looked down at
himself, suddenly confirming a long desired knowledge. As the expression faded, so too did his
surprise. Liam was curious, but he refrained, for he knew he himself would not share cherished
past so lightly with anyone so quickly either. He did know that something strange was about to
happen, something powerful.
“What was the news?” Liam questioned instead.
“It was not good. A… friend of mine has informed me that an ancient evil has control of
this land now, but that they have a last desperate plan to solve the… menacing Dark Fire, and his
opponent, to thwart them.”
“Opponent…,” Liam opened his mouth slightly, then without waiting for the man to
respond, “How do you know this, who is this ‘friend’?”
The man only sighed, but eventually broke the silence with a shocking answer, “I am the
hero who saved the hero, the man directing the past, and a messenger to the future. I have seen
the Dark Fire, whose name speeds the absence of light. I have seen the nation rise only to
crumble because of my doing, and in the great desert, its name scorching fear and dread into the
mind of all because of its twisted nature from which the creature of red and purple devour the
thrumming consciousness of one's mind; they serve that dark accursed fire. And when I draw
near, so too does the guardian of my most sacred and cherished act; he who was born of my
heritage, and he who will also save you from the swift current of time. Remember who I am, and
when you do, you will know that when hope is absent, look to the past, not seizing it, but to find
a solution, a chance at redemption to save those you hold close, to those you love; hope is never
lost, because inside us all there is a spark striking, a will, a desire, a burning fire to reckon the
forces of this world in their places; that power is the force built out of not quite preparation, nor
bravery, but an iron fisted will to believe in that which the wind will sweep away if you let your
grip falter. This I have learned, and this you will learn also. A message I give to prepare you for
the protopia that you can create…” the man's inflections ran wildly as he spoke.
“This is who I am,” the man almost whispered.
Liam was stunned, he could not even raise his eyebrows nor drop his jaw. His eyebrows
were furrowed slightly, eyes unblinking as he stared expressionless and hunched at the man. The
man stared back. Finally, Liam made a movement, opening his mouth and cocking his head to
the left, but no words came out. He shook his head and blinked several times to somewhat clear
his thoughts. It did little to help.
“I… know, it is a lot,” the man comforted.
“Well… uh, I couldn’t… I know but…” Liam laughed, “Yeah, I do not know.”
“I remember many things now, you are in the future.”
“How far, how do you know?” Liam begged.
“I have been in your past you know, I know what you did, have done, eh?” the man
chuckled
“When?” Liam pleaded
“A time before this one,” the man nodded slightly.
Giving up, Liam abandoned his quest to fish out the man’s identity and instead asked,
“Who is this friend?”
“Ah, someone I believe you have also heard about. He is not very experienced, but is very
wise, wiser than ever I was.”
“Wise?”
Chuckling a little the man replied, “Questions. Wisdom is not a matter of knowledge or
power, rather what you decide to do with them. Joy, true joy, is choosing the right task normally,
if you are wise, you have joy. Not to say we do not fall short, but that choosing the right time,
place, thing to act upon, brings joy”
“Hmm,” Liam wondered thoughtfully.
“I, that which a man chooses to look for will be that which he acts upon, or for… it be
that which he stands for. If you look for water, surely you must eventually find it. If you cannot,
that does not mean it is not there, that only means you must look longer. Though, if never you
find it, it is still there, always present, thrumming, living, flowing.”
“If death takes us before we reach it?”
“There is always, always, another chance.”
“How?”
“A man once told me, ‘Wisdom is not found in the distance traveled, but in where your
steps bend toward the truth. Even if you do not reach it in this life, the path itself prepares you
for the next.’”
The man got up to stoke the fire and add an unseen log to the fire. As Liam inspected the
darkness outside of the firelight, he wondered about the implications of the man’s statement. It
seemed vast to think about another chance. He then thought of his time in the tunnel, the lack
of hope he had felt, then remembered, ‘What is my life?’ What is my focus?
Suddenly, Liam made the conviction to serve his friends, though he felt a sorrow,
something deeper actually, that drove him into the mindset. He decided to be somewhat
vulnerable with the man, “I do not know where my friends are now, they could be chained to a
wall in a vast dungeon.”
“I know where they are, but you cannot find them unless you get back.”
“Do you know how,” Liam implored.
“In the past, the world is shifting. I sense an ache in time starting. He who is connected
to it also feels it.”
Liam looked at him with a confused expression, “How do I get back.”
“Do you feel it?”
“What?”
“The ache. Look. Listen.”
Liam sighed, thinking the man was crazy, but did as he was told. For clarity of thought,
he held his breath and closed his eyes. He tried to imagine what he was supposed to be feeling.
What was the man talking about?
He let out his first breath and then, he felt it, a dull throbbing in his mind, gentle, but
present, soft but hushed. It was a deeper clarity than he had ever had in his life. Something that
he had to look for. He breathed multiple times in quick succession as he realized what was
within him. ‘He who is connected to it also feels it.’ What, what? What was it? Liam’s question
went unanswered for a long time, then he heard a voice, Your power. You know what it has been all
your life. You have had a valid perception of time, as such, your talent is rewarded with power. Now that
you know this, do not brush it aside.
Without fully realizing he had opened his eyes, he came back to himself and found
himself laying on the floor, the realization had apparently tugged at his mind, though it was not
too much to bear. The man loomed over him and extended a hand for Liam to grasp. As Liam
reached, he could feel cold tears streak his face, and then the warmth of the fire.
Before Liam grasped his hand, he paused as the man said, “Thank you,” the man’s words
were confusing at first, but then Liam realized the man was thanking him.
“For what?”
“You have discovered the truth of yourself. You have learned to see that which is in front
of you, but not only this, but that which is not in your path.”
“What do you mean?”
“You turned away from that which you have held on to all your life, and seen that which
is waiting to be seen, Power.”
“Oh… how do I use it?”
“Many are here in this world to teach you. I also can teach you a few things regarding
this subject,” the man had a smirk on his face as he hoisted Liam up firmly, and then looked him
directly in the eyes, “I learned this from myself, but enough of this, let us eat. Whole you were
gone, a cooked that beautiful, colorful little fishy you caught for us. Again not a bad size.”
The man retreated to where he had been sitting, and motioned for Liam to take a nearby
seat. The seat was only a log, but it sufficed. Liam took the seat the man offered, and barely
before the man tore off a piece of the fish and waved it at him. The smell was good enough, and
Liam had just realized that it was on a dark wooden plate. An unnecessary thought went
through Liam’s head, Where did he get this wood from, I thought it was only pine here.
He looked around to confirm his idea, then took the plate with the fish on it. The fish
smelled… fishy, though had a slight hint of mintiness and lemon to it. Liam could clearly
recognize bits of salt as he opened up the fish fully gutted.
“It is a beautiful white and perfect fish,” the man commented.
Liam nodded, though he had never had fish before, so he did not know what a well
cooked fish was supposed to look like. He had no utensils, and as he looked over at the man, he
saw that he was just using his hands. Liam raised his eyebrows multiple times in quick
succession and then decided to eat. If the man saw, he did not comment.
He wanted to say something, but refrained, to upkeep the manners he chose.
The man finished before him, but remained silent until Liam was also finished. If he was
going to say anything, he hid it well, though his eyes stabbed at Liam until he was done.
“Afacianfiwfaci.”
Liam’s confused expression at what the man had just said was obvious on his face, and
he let it remain. The man laughed a little then said again, “The Forgotten Land made me forget.”
“Oh… ho,” Liam said slowly.
“What do you need?” The man looked sternly into his eyes, and the sudden change in his
demeanor made him wonder if the man was serious. When the man nodded and raised his
eyebrows, “Anything, knowledge, wisdom, advice?”
“Why is the land black? Why am I here? Who are you?” He narrowed his eyes on the last
question.
Evil takes Evil, Light takes Light. The enemy has corrupted it, they have endured
through all of this. Their allies are obliterated, even the ones they thought close and powerful.
Few remain. Time needs a new ruler and its leader needs a guardian.”
Liam narrowed his eyes even more since the man had shied away from answering the last
question. He refrained from asking again; instead he asked, “What advice would you part with?”
Another indirect answer from the man irritated Liam, “What is pain?”
“...A burn, a scratch a… well some sort of hurt?” Liam tried.
“No!” The man shouted as he got up and walked around, “Pain is only a perception. The
greatest warriors can get their arm severed, but they continue to fight. How?”
“They believe in their cause?”
“Aye, they are focused on fulfilling their duty, their focus is not on the pain, but the duty
they are commanded to fulfill. Look around, where are the trees?”
Confused at why the man was treating him so, he glanced at the trees dimly lit by the
fire, and pointed.
The man rolled his eyes, “Oh… How many leaves, how many branches, start counting.”
Liam growled slightly. He started counting the leaves on the trees only to be interrupted.
“Ah, see? Now that you are looking for them you see more clearly?”
Letting his anger flow out a bit, Liam growled, “What’s your point?”
“If we only see what’s right in front of us, what is it we learn? When we look about, we
see that which is not there to be seen. The small insignificant details can be the undoing, or
constructing, of greater things.”
“Why…” Liam was cut off.
“Often, the times we learn about the world are when we take the time to sit still after a
hard day.”
Liam sighed and looked at the sky like a little kid waiting for their folks to stop talking.
He could not see the stars, and he sensed a hushed wind. Not like the hush he had heard before,
he decided to calm down, open his ears to listen for something more.
The wind blew softly in his ears, the psithurism ruffled the leaves in the trees, and the
world slowed. The soft rumble of thunder could be heard as the fire suddenly spat an ember out
of the ring. The chirping of crickets could be heard somewhere making their music as well as
the clicking of a small bug beneath his feet. The fire popped again and he could hear the patient
rhythm of his breath, and the man’s, rolling in, then out. The thunder sounded again and the
wind blew a little harder, the smell of rain fresh in the air.
Lightning flowed across the sky and against the pale of the moon a curtain of rain fell
and the sky let go at the far end of the vale, rushing toward them like a wet blanket draping the
sky with its fluffy looking mist. The sight was out of a painting. They bore themselves off to the
hill’s entrance, letting the rain douse the fire. Under the thatched roof’s protection, they
watched the rain fall on the dirt. Then as the constant pattering of raindrops pelted the path,
they ducked inside.
29: Satisfactions
Inside, as Liam sat listening to the rain, he wondered about what the man had said, and
what the land would be like. His thoughts were like a mixed chorus of croaking frogs and
buzzing bees. He sat there, staring at his hand, his eyes rarely blinked.
The man was hunched by the fire he had started, stirring a thick gravy that was not yet
boiling. In a different pan, large puffy biscuits rose with a perfect golden brown covering them.
They were on the edge of the fire to stay warm, for they had already cooked in the small oven
above the fire.
Liam remembered the time he had first been in the hill, and the day seemed to drag
longer than it should have. He remembered the man's hospitality and overall bearing as he
talked and showed Liam around. It brought a small smile to Liam’s face; he hadn’t done that for
a while, at least it felt too long.
Liam’s mind then drifted to Nia and Andy. Where were they? What were they doing?
Were they safe? The questions and concern ran fast through his mind as he hung over the worry
for an uncertain amount of time, though it couldn’t have been long, because the man let out a
small sigh and stood up.
Taking the pan full of gravy first, and a hot pad that was sitting on the counter, he
brought it over to the table, followed by the biscuits. A few plates in proportion to the pans were
brought out of the cupboard, and they ate without words for a time.
Liam enjoyed the time of silence with just the good taste of well cooked simple food and
the sound of the rain. The food took his mind off the worry he had begun to accumulate in his
mind. They were both content with the food, and Liam wondered then about the man's ability
with time. The man had said he could teach Liam a bit about time power, but the prospect only
got Liam’s mind churning again with questions, so he finished his food before he asked any of
them.
When they had both finished, Liam before the man, Liam questioned the man, “Where
did you learn about your power, and what drove you to it?”
A broad smile unfurled across his face as the man answered, “Well, I was in the same
position as you. Confused, I even fainted, as you say you have,” he remarked with a smirk.
Liam shot a confused look at the man. He thought the man would not answer, but was
surprised when he did.
“I have been living in the past, quite literally, and I have only just remembered that I
have been here for twenty-three years. During that time, I have saved many men from a forgetful
history.”
He continued longer than expected, though very eager to share, “I saved a young man
from a deep fate. I knew what to help him with, a pale willow, bright and silvery like rain
descending on a sunny day. Now, he will save the world, and it was I who made all of it possible,
and it was he who made it possible for my acts,” the man appeared somber.
“Who was he?” Liam asked, curious.
“He was a brave man, a funny one, one who always held to his morals and looked to
improve their boundaries. He had great potential, and I knew him twice.”
Liam decided not to question the man farther, for his expression was deepening,
eyebrows furrowed as if he hung in profound thought. Liam let silence prevail for a while before
a slow throbbing in his head began suddenly. The throb seemed to match his pulse, but he was
not sure, and with every throb, old memories returned. The wonder and awe at meeting Andy
again. His promises, and his broken ones. Long lost memories of his mother and father.
The thoughts continued endlessly, and the man seemed to be experiencing the same
when Liam glimpsed his face through the short openings in the mirage of his thoughts.
It seemed as if time was slipping away from him, like he was taken back to the times he
had been recalling.
It stopped, and he saw the man's thumb and middle finger come out of a snap. His face
was serious, and he looked Liam directly in the eyes.
“I have to get you back to your time, but there is one thing remaining for you to do. I do
not know how much time we have, so let's snap to.”
“What, what is it?”
“There is something I want you to have.”
The man threw himself out of his chair as he said the words, and the chair clattered to
the ground. Liam followed as the man rushed out the door. Liam was about to ask where the
man was going, but the door was slammed shut flatly in his face in the man’s haste.
Liam let out a small groan and staggered back a few inches before returning his hand to
the door handle to open it. When he was out the door, Liam saw the man on his hands and knees
hunched over the pond. He seemed to be looking for something almost like he would a wild
animal. The awareness that he had adopted was that needed of a hunter in uncharted wilderness,
wild and dangerous.
Liam approached the man, and inquired of his actions.
The confusing but expected response came, “You need the power of the blade.”
“Is it down there?”
“Aye, it once belonged to the Warrior, caught in his anguish of naught.”
“How do we get it?”
The man pushed himself onto his knees saying, “Go down and get it, I will tell you that
which you need to know after.”
“Uh… how deep is it,” Liam stammered cautiously, but confidently.
“I have never been so inclined to figure that out,” the man paused, “look, you can see a
yellow-blue pulse,” the man inclined his head toward the pond’s dark waters.
Liam glanced toward the pond, trying to discern the glow the man had mentioned, but
the shattered refraction of the stars on the rippling pond obscured an easy image. Suddenly, the
breeze that stirred the pond ceased, and he could make out the color of yellow and blue
alternating pulses.
Liam looked at the man, and the man nodded, “The answers lie waiting within the hilt.”
Confused at what the man meant by the hilt, he hesitated and looked into the man’s eyes.
He appeared to be completely serious, and the look that resided on his face betrayed no
impression of irony. The man nodded, and looked Liam directly back in the eyes, “Go.”
Liam jumped onto the grey sand below him, and removed his traveling boots, that he
realized he must not have taken off when he fell asleep. He must have been too tired. He also
removed his belt, but nothing else, only the heaviest things. His socks were worn thin and had
holes in the heel. In this manner, Liam waded out into the dark lake.
Without looking back at the man, for he knew what the man would say, Liam
accustomed himself to the water. It was warm on the surface, but cold beneath.
He prepared himself for what he was about to do, fixing his eyes on the yellow-blue
throb. He had never swam before, at least not in something this deep, though he could hold his
breath for an impressive amount of time. Sometimes, he would practice it while thinking on his
own, it was hard, and it was something he had to develop.
He continued to drag his legs through the water, until the water was to his shoulders,
and a large drop-off appeared as a murky line in the water. Summoning his courage, he looked
through the foggy water at the pulse… and dove.
The water was much colder than the shallow water now behind him. The water tugged at
his clothes, as if slippery fingers were trying to drag him back. His nose filled with water, but he
was most concerned about his supply of air. Liam found that his air was harder to keep
underwater, and it felt like he was losing more. Still he pumped his legs and paddled. It got
dimmer and darker, while the soft pulse grew brighter and sharper in his vision.
His eyes against the chilling water required him to blink more frequently. The pulse was
large now, illuminating the small particles floating in gray murk. Scarce rays from the moon
filtered through the water in slow ripples. The light of the hilt flowed across his vision, and
Liam hit the bottom of the lake. His hand stirred the mud and threw it about. Some of it got in
his eyes, but the blue-yellow pulsed even though his eyes were closed to fend off the tiny
particles. He fumbled around for the light, to find its location and seize it. His head was
throbbing and darkness creeped in from the edges of his vision.
His hand bumped something hard, but when he returned his hand to where he thought
the object was, it fell on only more muddy earth. His hand felt a small sting, though his mind
barely felt the pain, for the pain was focused on the pounding in his head.
He closed his hand around the cold steel and then followed it to the hilt. His eyes
opened, and as he raised his hand, the pulsing color raised with it. Relieved, yet hard-pressed,
Liam felt his air slipping away quickly and he pushed himself off the lake floor.
As he floated slowly back towards the fractured moonlit land, the only sensation present
was the reverberating beats of his heart through his head as his blood strove to keep him
conscious. Faster and faster it pumped, until Liam wondered if he was unconscious, but then his
mind slipped away from him, and he knew not what happened after.
***
His dreams ran slowly in and out, following a slow heartbeat in the background he could not see.
The beat seemed only once or twice per minute and Liam now was conscious of his dreams. He
remembered vaguely, as if his thoughts were underwater.
The dreams were of his past, decisions he could’ve made differently, mistakes he had made. They
brought all manner of emotions to his remembrance, as if he had never felt them before. They shifted then
to the recent events of the past two days, events that he could never have anticipated. His thoughts and
consciousness drifted through the fishing, the first time he had woken up in the glade, and the warm soup.
He remembered the rain and the sight over the mountains, and suddenly, a realization struck him.
The wall. The wave. The water. That great mountainous barrier, obscuring the view of the other
side. What was behind, what could possibly lie behind?
The thought was swept away, and the faces of Nia, Andy, and himself took its place. Suddenly an
involuntary question pervaded his mind, echoing all through his mind. It came in the form of mystery, as
if talking to an old, wise person, confusing.
Of the whispers in the trees
And the roaring of the seas
With the biting of the breeze
And sorrow and disease
In silence and peace is it found
To listen, discern the sound
Here is it found
Not deep underground
Nor over wall
But after the fall
Here we may see
Here’s where it be
Follow the note
To this we devote
And seek for them in need
From west to east
There lies the feast
That ever so silently called
Follow it close
Keep it
For you never know
Who it’s effects might befall
Who?
With that, even the silence ceased, and nothing but blackness remained.
***
When he rose, the first thing he was aware of was a heat and throb in his head. He put
his hand to his head, pulling it through the blankets he was wrapped in. Then, everything he felt
and heard gained more definition as the remnants of sleep trance vanished. He sat up. The
sounds of twittering birds and of the waterwheel pumping the water flowed in from the open
door, and from a window he had previously not seen. A smooth breeze followed the sound, and
it felt invigorating. Even though the cold pulled the warmth out of his exposed arms, Liam was
also aware of the comfort beneath the blanket. All the sounds and feelings Liam experienced
gave him a sense of peace.
Upon further inspection of his surroundings, he realized he was not in the hill at all, but
rather in a small cabin, or at least a room. The open door did not reveal the same view as from
the hill-home.
Liam stood up, taking in the morning’s delights, and through the window, the yellow sun
filtered through the window, momentarily blinding him. Outside, under a small awning , the
trees looked greener and the grass had a layer of dew covering it, some of the droplets resting on
the grass reflected sharp rays of sunlight. There was not a deck following the awning out, but
only a small step up to the cabin
His feet felt wet, and only then did he take the time to examine himself. His socks were
soaked, the white of his socks a contrast to the healthy grass beneath them. He was now wearing
a baggy, long sleeved tunic, stretching at least a foot past his waist, and comfortable brown
pants that felt silky on the inside. They looked brand new.
The little cabin rested on a hill, and it was no more than seven or eight feet long — less
wide. To Liam, the cabin seemed to be resting on the foothills of the mountain bowl, for he
could look out across the trees. He could even see a small billowing smoke trail that must have
come from the chimney in the hill.
Everything was so bright out in the bowl, and even above. The sky was a hue of baby
blue, and long, tall, fluffy clouds blew noticeably across the horizon to the left. The clouds closer
to the rising sun were illuminated like molten gold, and the clouds below like waves, pushing
themselves off the mountains.
Liam let out a short sigh and was about to say something, but the view was too
captivating, majestic — a perfect day.
Liam walked back inside the small cabin to see if his worn boots had been brought as
well. Upon searching for them, he instead found a new pair of boots that looked the exact same
as his when they had been new, resting on wooden posts upside-down. A small piece of paper
read,
Anything you want in here is yours.
I have some things to do, so don’t cause trouble.
I will likely be back when the sun rises over the hill.
-Your Friend
Folding the paper and placing it in his pocket, Liam pulled the boots on and looked
around the cabin. There was not much, but there was a grey tunic hanging by the bed, and upon
a small table an unlit candle and some papers. Leaning against the table, was the sword.
Swirling gold on the hilt climbed to the guard, set with the beautiful blue-yellow gem on the
very pommel. It had lost its pulsing light, but it remained just as beautiful.
He had nowhere to be, and frankly, he didn’t know where to go if he could.
He strode over to where the sword lay, and examined it. He bent down to touch the gem,
but caught sight of another small paper on the table. It was short and quickly scratched with the
same long letters on the note by the boots.
Take it, the scabbard lies under
the floorboards under the bed.
Liam moved the sword and bent down to look under the table, note still in hand.
Reaching his right hand under the wooden framed bed, Liam pressed his fingers on the
floorboards, testing their stability. Finally, one rattled and he found the groove and reached his
thin fingers underneath it.
Dropping the note, he let his left hand keep the floorboard suspended and fished his
right in the hole underneath. He felt a smooth leathery surface, cold and hard and guessed it was
the scabbard. Wrapping his fingers around it, he pulled it up, and examined the design. There
were thin vines stamped into the leather, maintaining the same thickness weaving around the
leather's surface and a small silvery cap on the end of the scabbard.
The Sword itself gave off a sheen when he turned it just right, and he put the sword in its
rightful place When the sword reached the end of its sheath with a dull thud.
Walking down to the hill, Liam took the sword with him, remembering that if he was
going to leave soon, he had to be ready. He wanted to be there when the sun rose over the hill.
Though he had only been there for a short time, the feeling that he needed to be somewhere was
weighing on his mind.
On his walk down, his new boots prevented his socks from getting soggy more than they
were. They were still causing problems, and at one point he slipped, but he accomplished the
desired task, got to the bottom, though it seemed a longer way than it was.
Upon reaching the bottom and setting the sword by the door to the hill and wandered to
the back of the hill. He sat by the stream and listened, watching the water fumble over the rocks
and watched as the waterwheel turned. Eventually, he got bored of watching the water and
started aimlessly meandering around the territory. He even returned to the spot he had
originally woke up, in the glade.
Sitting down, Liam enjoyed a few moments listening to the same sounds, and wondered
about the past. About how they had gotten so far so fast. It all seemed un-natural. However, the
more he thought about it, the more his head really started to hurt. He wanted to leave as soon as
possible, he needed to know where Nia and Andy were. It was weighing heavily on his mind, for
even though he knew not where they were, he felt a pull to follow.
He waited for only a little longer, and finally, the man showed. He came from behind the
hill, looking for Liam. The man was rushed, seemingly possessing some new information that
fueled his urgency. Liam ran to the man and asked him what he had done. Liam too had reason
for worry.
“I had to help a friend.”
“The one you were talking about.”
The man only nodded and then, “come on.”
Liam followed without question as the man wove his way over the little paths to the hill.
Upon arriving, he took up the sword and gave it to Liam.
“Take this with you, I will not be able to teach you, but through this, others can.”
“What will you be doing?”
“...Helping. From afar,” a mischievous smirk melted onto his face.
Liam nodded slowly, and then nodded again, “How does this work?”
The man nodded in return, and stepped closer, looking down at the sword he grasped in
his hands. Liam took it, feeling the cold steel on his skin. It gave him a shiver. It almost burned,
and the man watched closely as Liam’s eyes widened, and a strange sensation rippled through
him.
“Listen close, you will need to know these meanings.”
Liam listened.
“O-a-na-ri. And the Light Returns. Ri-ar. Come back — please,” the man spelled out,
emphasizing every sound, making sure to say them right, “now say them together, and in
whatever fashion, show that you completed the command. I will feed you energy in order for you
to complete it, but you have to know the true meaning of the sounds before you can accomplish
the same on your own.”
Liam nodded, and sighed, then… spoke.
“Oanari… riar.”
He thought of what way to complete it, then he snapped his fingers and all at once, the
world blurred around him, and he shifted as if something was forcing him back. The last thing
he saw was the man's outstretched hand, in farewell.
30: Like The Shattering of Glass
He regained his consciousness, and the clearing he was in appeared very similar to the
one he had appeared in. The clearing was fresh with spring leaves and a smell of decaying litter.
Something had roused the smell and stirred the dust. He stood, but now, the world seemed off,
so very uncomfortable, and he fell forward the moment his legs straightened. Something had
pushed him, but nothing was behind him. The world seemed to have a pressure to it, like
something Liam was suddenly aware of.
The sensation of traveling through the confused blur returned, and he remembered the
man, and his cryptic comments. But the sensation was also out of place, why was it back, he was
not traveling back in time. His wonder was short lived, not that he could think about it either,
because the pressure was relieved.
The world broke.
Liam fell on his face, and he felt like he had been shattered into a billion pieces. The
sound of glass breaking and the leaves swiftly shifted their color, from yellow, to orange, to a
bright, beautiful red. It was the color of Nia’s hair. A strange thought entered Liam’s mind, the
thought of power and of death, a nonsensical image of a cat swallowing a sparrow, but the
sparrow within forgetfulness, but neither moved, as if they forgot their purpose, that the bird
should be flying away, and the cat eating its meal, then that too broke.
And the wind blew through the trees. And the leaves remained. Liam squinted in
confusion.
“I have to find them.”
He said it out loud, and he meant it, remembering the words he had heard when his
dreams flew wild lying in the cabin. From west to east.
East, Liam thought, then worded, “That is where I need to go.”
Looking for any possible sign of the direction east was, Liam glanced at the ground,
nothing there.
He looked at the sky, the leaves were too thick.
He looked forward, and a footprint in the sand showed him his sword, and a path
forward through the tangle.
31: A Test
Nia walked slowly through the swaying trees. Under the thinning canopy, she was
unsure if she was walking in the right direction. She wasn’t even sure what the right direction
was. If she could find a river, she could follow that, but thirst was clawing at her. If she didn’t
find water, the natural course of all living things would befall her.
She had only been wandering about a day, and already, it seemed the sun was starting to
set. The leaves grew darker as the sun ceased to show, and the green underbrush was fading
from distinct detail. Beneath the canopy’s veil, the scape was fairly clear for the most part, but
every now and then, a thick bramble would hinder her progress. Walking through the forest at
dusk was enchanting, the sun shining across the already yellow and red tinted leaves.
She knew she would have to find a place to stop for the night, but she was eager to push
on. Now in the darkening night, she suddenly felt very tired. From her pack, for she had
recovered it, she pulled her only blanket. Only a little food was in there, and her water was
drained a little while ago.
When she had woken up in the forest, Andy was gone, and Liam was nowhere to be
found, not even a bootprint from Liam’s boots. She had followed the faint and fading trail Andy
left behind, though a touch of sadness lingered with her in leaving Liam.
Taking the blanket and spreading it on the ground, she prepared to lay down and get
what rest she could. As she lay there, she thought about Andy, and wondered as she had for a
few days about the past. Now, it seemed so transparent, but foggy, like looking through a
descending curtain of rain. The darkness slowly creeped in, but she remained awake for a time
wondering then what would become of her friends, not just Andy and Liam, but the Oakenash
and the soldiers from Aurumpraess.
***
Through the thick silence of morning, a single chirp floated down from the canopy to
meet Nia’s ears. The clearing was bright with the sun's golden rays trickling through the tinted
foliage.
She could feel something off about the forest — the rustle of leaves, and the feeling of an
unknown presence. Casting her eyes around the clearing, she saw an old man leaning against a
fat ash, one who looked tattered and torn through many days of travel. A long silvery beard
wagged at his waist but his face betrayed no sign of old age. His face was dark and stretched, but
an inquisitive expression was painted on his face.
“What’s your name,” Nia asked in as smooth of a voice she could.
“I am a guardian and a guide,” was the only reply that came in so monotone a voice
nothing could be discerned in his feelings, “you may call me… Theron… for now.”
“Okay.”
Staying about twenty feet away, Theron said, “What do you plan to do? To go, I mean.”
“What? Oh, uh, I am looking for a friend,” she said it so softly, hoping her fear would not
penetrate too much.
“Hmm, one place many have found the most precious things close to them is an easterly
direction, a willow of pale light.”
“Where?”
“Well if you’ll come with me.”
Oh, well… I wonder if he’s actually leading me somewhere. Or just playing me. Still, he looks like
he is nice. After a little more consideration she sighed, and followed the man out of the clearing,
snatching her blankets and pack as she did.
On they went, through the forest, but if she quickened her pace, the man matched it,
always maintaining his distance. The land wherever he walked seemed to bend for him to pass
easily, but whenever a tangle of underbrush came into the path, no observable change was made,
and there was a different, easier path. It made Nia wonder if the man had powers, or if some
enchantment was upon him, helping him find a clear path.
They walked almost without pausing, but Nia’s legs started to hurt from the amount she
had done. The ache was spreading to a part in her arm, whatever this one was from, but the man
seemed to know. The man stopped in the middle of two trees that had grown out of a previously
standing one. The bark underneath his feet was rotted and returning back to the dirt it had
grown out of. Theron then turned around and said, “Rest if you can, we’ve many hours before
the sun sets, and your friend will likely still be on the move.”
Though she was grateful, she wondered how Theron had known her need, or if it was
only coincidence. She sat on the top of a nearby boulder. There were many large rocks around
her and some of the trees grew out of them, resilient and relentless.
When they finally weren’t running into numerous hindrances, the sun was setting in the
west. They had finished off the day, and already, they had traveled, they presumed, about seven
leagues. It was a very acceptable distance. With a sense of accomplished satisfaction, they sat to
rest as again the fine dust could be seen as the light filtered through the roof of bright-painted
leaves. It was strange to think about the way the trees seemed to listen, waiting for something. It
was like humbly waiting to see the results of some test the trees were giving.
The test reminded her of the way Andy could talk to animals, and she often forgot, she
now could too. The man watched the forest, looking around as if there was an unseen detail or
something that had been before, that now wasn’t. Meanwhile she continued to ponder on the
power she had, and the patience of the trees. She wondered if Liam might have some power too,
if maybe he just didn’t know about it, just like she hadn’t known until Andy questioned her.
She wondered these things as she glanced through the thinning canopy. Through a small
gap, she saw three stars right beside each other, all of them twinkling brightly, save one which
flickered sporadically. She sighed multiple times, thinking, wondering, pondering what the
world would be like in the future. What would happen to them, all of them?
Finally, she prepared to sleep, laying her blankets out gently and laying upon them, as
the heat seeped from the night. As the warmth dissolved, and the soft breeze blew in, she pulled
her blanket over her and continued to think until she could fall asleep.
Glancing at where the man had been, she could no longer see him. Though she briefly
looked around, she could not see the man, and no light whatsoever could be seen through the
trees. It remained this way, till the moon came out, but Nia soon drifted into a dream, that of
which was vague and blurry as most dreams were. She could barely remember the last part when
she woke up.
In the trees, she looked around the corner, then saw a willow of tangled and stretched
proportions. She only remembered wondering where it had been seen before.
With her fading dreams, Nia woke slowly, the chill greeting her cheeks, and her copper
hair strung across her face. What was that? But even as she tried to recall the strangeness of the
dream, it slipped away. She blew her hair out of her face, and already the leaves that had turned
so quickly had fallen during the night’s soft wind. The yellow leaves clung to her hair when she
stood up, and already, the canopy appeared to be half as thick as it was the night before. The
leaves were more rusty, and they were crinkled further than the night before.
The man was sitting against the same tree she had last seen him, and the soft rise and
fall of his arms on his chest marked that he was still alive. She could not tell if he was conscious
or not.
She stood up and started to walk towards Theron, but he opened his eyes and rolled
away, standing up after. Nia stopped, wondering why he didn’t want her near.
“No, you don't want to do that,” he said with wariness.
She was confused, why not? The first thing she could think of would be some disease
that might spread to her. Instead of just leaving it like a log, though, she decided to voice it.
“Is there something wrong with you,” it sounded weird the way she said it, but no other
form of the question came as she thought.
“I am not to be touched, I do not want the taint that is upon me to spread,” his voice was
urgent but smooth and practiced.
Nia sighed, but she decided not to question it, mostly because she didn’t want to offend
the man by further question or curiosity. “Where must we go?”
The man loosened and straightened but even he seemed to be aware of the time. He
gestured to her, bags reminding her to take them and bring them. It would be hard to forget, but
it seemed like a courtesy — to wait for his action or give permission first.
She retrieved the bags with all her belongings and waited for the man. He watched the
forests closely before setting off once more.
